@plainvoice true

=== par-01 | Why parallel: the end of free speed, Amdahl's law and Gustafson's law
--- teach
In Operating Systems you met threads: several flows of execution inside one process, sharing its memory. There they were mostly a way to overlap waits, so one thread computes while another waits for the disk. This course is about the other reason for threads: using many processors at once to finish a job **sooner**. It starts with two questions. Why did the whole industry have to learn this? And how much faster can many processors possibly make a program? The answer to the second question is a formula you will use for the rest of the course.

### Free speed, and how it ended

For about thirty years, programs got faster without anyone changing them. Every two years or so, a new chip ran the same code nearly twice as fast. Two trends made that happen.

- **[[Moore's law|moore]]**: the number of transistors that fit on a chip doubles about every two years. It is an observation about manufacturing, not a law of nature.
- **[[Dennard scaling|dennard]]**: when transistors shrink, they also need less voltage and switch faster, so a chip can run at a higher clock rate while using the same power per square millimetre.

Around 2005 the second trend stopped. The power a chip burns grows roughly like **P ≈ C · V² · f**: the capacitance being switched (C), times the voltage squared (V²), times the clock frequency (f). Voltage could no longer drop, because transistors at very low voltage leak current even when they are off. So a higher clock rate meant more heat than a chip can shed. This is the **power wall**. Clock rates stalled at around 3 to 5 GHz, where they still are.

Moore's law kept going for another fifteen years, so chips still got more transistors. Designers spent them on **more cores**: several complete processors on one chip. Here is why that is a good trade. Run two cores at 80% of the clock rate. The lower rate lets the voltage drop to about 80% too, so each core burns about 0.8 × 0.8² × 0.8 ≈ 0.41 of the power of one full-speed core, and two of them burn about 0.82. Two slower cores together can do 1.6 times the work of one fast core, for less power. But only if the program keeps both busy. A program with one thread gets nothing from the second core. Herb Sutter summed it up in 2005: "the free lunch is over". Speed now has to be written into the program.

### Speedup and efficiency

To talk about "faster" precisely, name the times:

- **T₁**: the time the job takes on one processor.
- **Tₚ**: the time it takes on p processors.

The **speedup** on p processors is **Sₚ = T₁ / Tₚ**: how many times faster p processors are than one. The **efficiency** is **Eₚ = Sₚ / p**: the fraction of the machine that did useful work.

A job that takes 120 seconds on one core and 40 seconds on 4 cores has speedup 120 / 40 = 3 and efficiency 3 / 4 = 0.75. **Linear speedup** means Sₚ = p (efficiency 1): every processor pulls its full weight. It is the ideal, and it is rare. Speedup above p, [[superlinear speedup|superlinear]], happens occasionally, and it always has a special cause.

### Amdahl's law

Almost every program has parts that cannot be split. It reads a configuration file, sets up its data, or writes one final report. Call the time those take on one processor the **serial part**, and the rest the **parallel part**: work that divides evenly among any number of processors.

Say a fraction **s** of the one-processor time T₁ is serial, so a fraction 1 − s is parallel. On p processors:

- the serial part still takes s · T₁, because only one processor can do it;
- the parallel part takes (1 − s) · T₁ / p, because p processors share it perfectly.

So Tₚ = T₁ · (s + (1 − s) / p), and dividing T₁ by that gives **Amdahl's law**:

**Sₚ = 1 / (s + (1 − s) / p)**

Take an image-stacking job of 6 minutes on one core, of which 1 minute is serial (loading and aligning the frames). Then s = 1/6.

| cores p | Tₚ = 1 + 5/p minutes | speedup 6 / Tₚ |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | 6 | 1 |
| 4 | 2.25 | 2.67 |
| 16 | 1.3125 | 4.57 |
| 1,000 | 1.005 | 5.97 |

Even a thousand cores give less than 6 times the speed. That is not bad luck. It is a bound.

### Why the bound holds

The claim: **for every p, Sₚ < 1/s when s > 0, and Sₚ increases with p towards 1/s.**

1. The parallel term (1 − s) / p is positive for every p (when s < 1), so s + (1 − s) / p is **greater than** s.
2. Dividing 1 by something bigger than s gives something smaller than 1/s. So Sₚ < 1/s.
3. As p grows, (1 − s) / p shrinks towards 0, so the denominator falls towards s and Sₚ rises towards 1/s. It never gets there, because the denominator is never exactly s.

In words: even if the parallel part took no time at all, the serial part would still take s · T₁, so the job can never be more than T₁ / (s · T₁) = 1/s times faster. A program that is 5% serial can never run more than 20 times faster, on any number of processors. Gene Amdahl made this argument in 1967, against machines built from many processors.

### How many processors for a target speedup

Amdahl's law can be turned around. Say you want speedup at least t. Then you need

1 / (s + (1 − s) / p) ≥ t, which is the same as s + (1 − s) / p ≤ 1/t (flip both sides, which is allowed because both are positive).

Subtract s: (1 − s) / p ≤ 1/t − s. If the right side is 0 or negative, **no** number of processors works: t is at or above the bound 1/s. Otherwise multiply out: p ≥ (1 − s) / (1/t − s).

For the image job (s = 1/6) and a target of 4: p ≥ (5/6) / (1/4 − 1/6) = (5/6) / (1/12) = 10. Ten cores give exactly speedup 4, and nine give 6 / (1 + 5/9) ≈ 3.86. A target of 6 or more is impossible.

That boundary case, where the answer is exactly a whole number, is where floating-point numbers trip you up: 1/6 has no exact binary form, so the float calculation may come out as 10.000000000000002 and round up to 11. When the inputs are whole numbers, you can keep every step exact. Python's **[[fractions.Fraction|fraction]]** holds a rational number exactly, as a numerator and a denominator:

```python
from fractions import Fraction

s = Fraction(1, 6)
t = 4
print((1 - s) / (Fraction(1, t) - s))     # 10
print(0.1 + 0.2 == 0.3)                   # False
print(Fraction(1, 10) + Fraction(2, 10) == Fraction(3, 10))   # True
```

Or multiply the inequality out so that only whole numbers appear. With serial time a and parallel time b (so T₁ = a + b and s = a / T₁), speedup at least t on p processors means T₁ ≥ t · (a + b/p), that is **p · (T₁ − t·a) ≥ t · b**.

### Reading the serial fraction from a measurement

You rarely know s in advance. You can measure it. Run on p processors, measure Sₚ, and solve Amdahl's law for s:

**s = (1/Sₚ − 1/p) / (1 − 1/p)**, for p ≥ 2.

This measured value is called the **[[Karp–Flatt metric|karp-flatt]]**. If a program has speedup 6 on 8 cores, s = (1/6 − 1/8) / (1 − 1/8) = (1/24) / (7/8) = 1/21, about 0.048. Measure at several p. If the value stays the same, the program really has that much serial work. If it **grows** with p, something else is going on: an overhead that grows with the number of processors.

### Overheads: when more processors make it slower

Amdahl's law is optimistic. Real parallel programs pay costs that grow with p: starting threads, splitting the data, combining the results, waiting for each other. A simple model adds a cost c for each processor beyond the first:

Tₚ = a + b/p + c · (p − 1)

where a is the serial time and b the parallel time on one processor. The b/p term falls as p grows, and the c · (p − 1) term rises. So there is a best p, and beyond it adding processors makes the job **slower**. Going from p to p + 1 helps exactly when b/p − b/(p + 1) > c, that is when b / (p(p + 1)) > c. For b = 900 seconds and c = 1 second, that holds up to p = 29 (900 / (29 · 30) ≈ 1.03 > 1) and fails from p = 30 on (900 / (30 · 31) ≈ 0.97), so 30 processors is best. That is close to √(b/c) = 30, which is where the best p always lands in this model.

### Gustafson's law

Amdahl's law answers one question: **this** job, how much faster? In practice, people often use a bigger machine differently. They keep the time fixed and solve a bigger problem: a finer weather grid, more frames, a longer simulation. John Gustafson pointed out in 1988 that for those jobs the outlook is much better.

Measure on the parallel machine instead. On p processors, let a fraction **s′** of the run's time be serial and 1 − s′ parallel. On one processor, the serial part takes the same time, but the parallel part, which kept p processors busy, would take p times as long. So, with the parallel run taking time 1:

- one processor takes s′ + p · (1 − s′);
- p processors take s′ + (1 − s′) = 1.

The **scaled speedup** is their ratio, **Gustafson's law**:

**Sₚ = s′ + p · (1 − s′) = p − s′ · (p − 1)**

On 64 processors, with 5% of the parallel run spent in serial code, the scaled speedup is 64 − 0.05 × 63 = 60.85. It grows without limit as p grows.

### The two laws do not disagree

They look like opposites: one says speedup stops at 1/s, the other that it grows forever. But they measure the serial fraction on different runs. Amdahl's s is a fraction of the one-processor time; Gustafson's s′ is a fraction of the p-processor time. For the same job on p processors, the serial time is s′ in the parallel run and s′ + p(1 − s′) in total one-processor time, so

s = s′ / (s′ + p · (1 − s′)).

In the 64-processor example, s = 0.05 / 60.85 ≈ 0.00082. Put that into Amdahl's law with p = 64 and you get 60.85 again. The same job, the same speedup. The laws differ only in what they hold fixed as p changes: Amdahl holds the problem size fixed, which engineers call **[[strong scaling|scaling]]**; Gustafson holds the serial time fixed and lets the problem grow, which is **weak scaling**.

### Where it is used

- **Buying hardware.** Before paying for 128 cores, measure s. If the job is 10% serial, 16 cores already give 6.4 of the 10 times it can ever reach.
- **Finding what to fix.** Amdahl's law says the serial part dominates as p grows, so that is where optimisation pays. Reading one big input file with one thread is a classic serial bottleneck.
- **Supercomputers** are judged on weak scaling: a climate model runs a finer grid on a bigger machine in the same wall-clock time.
- **Mission operations.** Processing a night's telemetry before the morning pass is a fixed-deadline job: Gustafson's question. Replaying one anomaly faster is a fixed-size job: Amdahl's.

**Watch out:**

- **Mixing up the two fractions.** A profile of the parallel run gives s′, not s. Putting s′ into Amdahl's formula badly underestimates the speedup.
- **Assuming speedup equals p.** Even 1% serial work caps speedup at 100.
- **Comparing against the wrong T₁.** Speedup should be measured against the best one-processor program, not the parallel program run with one thread, which is often slower because of its extra bookkeeping.
- **Floats at the boundary.** When the answer to "how many processors?" lands exactly on a whole number, float rounding can push it one too high. Keep whole-number inputs exact.

::: context moore A doubling, not a law
In 1965 Gordon Moore, who later co-founded Intel, noticed that the number of components on a chip had doubled every year, and predicted it would continue. In 1975 he revised the rate to every two years. The industry then used it as a target, planning each new manufacturing process to hit it. The Intel 4004 of 1971 had about 2,300 transistors. A large chip today has tens of billions. The doubling has slowed in the 2020s, because transistors are now only a few dozen atoms across.
:::

::: context dennard Why shrinking once meant faster
In 1974 Robert Dennard and his colleagues at IBM showed that if every dimension of a transistor shrinks by a factor k, its voltage and current can shrink by k too. Then each transistor uses k² times less power, and k² more of them fit in the same area, so the power per square millimetre stays the same, while the smaller transistors switch k times faster. That is why each generation could raise the clock rate for free. When voltage stopped falling in the mid-2000s, the power per square millimetre started rising with every shrink instead.
:::

::: context superlinear Faster than p times faster
Sometimes p processors run a job more than p times faster. It seems impossible, but there is always a special reason. The usual one is memory: split a 64 MB data set over 8 processors, and each piece of 8 MB may fit in that processor's cache, so every access becomes much faster than on one processor, which had to keep fetching from main memory. Another is search: several processors exploring different branches may find the answer early. A superlinear result is a clue that the one-processor version was held back by something, not a new law.
:::

::: context fraction Exact rational numbers
A `Fraction` is a number stored as a whole-number numerator and denominator, always in lowest terms, so `Fraction(2, 4)` is `Fraction(1, 2)`. Adding, subtracting, multiplying and dividing Fractions gives exact Fractions, and they compare correctly with whole numbers: `Fraction(10, 1) == 10` is `True`. They are slower than floats, so they suit small calculations where an exact answer matters, like deciding which side of a boundary a value falls on. `math.ceil` works on them too: `math.ceil(Fraction(7, 2))` is 4.
:::

::: context karp-flatt Measuring the serial fraction
Alan Karp and Horace Flatt proposed this measurement in 1990. Its value is that it separates two reasons a program scales badly. Real serial code gives the same value at every p. Overheads that grow with p, such as more messages to exchange or more threads waiting at a lock, show up as a value that rises with p. So a table of the metric at 2, 4, 8 and 16 processors tells you whether to hunt for serial code or for overhead.
:::

::: context scaling Strong and weak scaling
The two curves below are for the same program with 5% serial work. Strong scaling (fixed problem size, Amdahl) flattens out: 15.4 at 64 processors, and never more than 20 however many you add. Weak scaling (problem grows with p, Gustafson) keeps climbing almost as fast as p.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 320 200" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <line x1="40" y1="170" x2="300" y2="170" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <line x1="40" y1="170" x2="40" y2="15" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="170" y="192" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">processors p (0 to 64)</text>
  <text x="14" y="95" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44" transform="rotate(-90 14 95)">speedup</text>
  <line x1="40" y1="170" x2="296" y2="18" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-dasharray="4 3"/>
  <text x="250" y="40" font-size="10" fill="#6c7a93">linear, p</text>
  <polyline fill="none" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2" points="40,170 44,167.6 56,161.7 72,155.9 104,148.3 136,143.5 168,140.2 232,136.0 296,133.4"/>
  <text x="296" y="126" font-size="10" text-anchor="end" fill="#1d6fd1">strong: 15.4 at 64, never 20</text>
  <polyline fill="none" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="2" points="40,170 44,167.6 56,160.9 72,151.8 104,133.8 136,115.7 168,97.7 232,61.6 296,25.5"/>

```
:::
--- task
A profile of a program, measured on **one** core, is a list of phases `(seconds, kind)`, where `seconds` is a whole number (0 or more) and `kind` is `"serial"` or `"parallel"`. Serial phases take the same time on any number of cores. Parallel phases divide perfectly among the cores. At least one phase has more than 0 seconds.

Write four functions:

- `time_on(profile, p)`: the predicted time on `p` cores (p ≥ 1), as a float.
- `speedup(profile, p)`: the predicted speedup T₁ / Tₚ on `p` cores, as a float.
- `speedup_limit(profile)`: the speedup that no number of cores can reach, 1/s, as a float. If the serial time is 0, return `float("inf")`.
- `cores_needed(profile, target)`: the **smallest** whole number p ≥ 1 whose speedup is at least `target`, or `None` if no number of cores gets there. `target` is a positive whole number or a `Fraction`. The answer must be exact, even when the speedup lands exactly on the target, and it must be fast even when the answer is enormous: one check needs 10¹⁸ cores.

For example, for `[(3, "serial"), (27, "parallel")]`, `time_on(..., 9)` is `6.0`, `speedup(..., 9)` is `5.0`, `speedup_limit(...)` is `10.0`, and `cores_needed(..., 5)` is `9`.
--- starter
import math
from fractions import Fraction


def time_on(profile, p):
    return 0.0


def speedup(profile, p):
    return 1.0


def speedup_limit(profile):
    return 1.0


def cores_needed(profile, target):
    return None
--- solution
import math
from fractions import Fraction


def split(profile):
    serial = sum(sec for sec, kind in profile if kind == "serial")
    parallel = sum(sec for sec, kind in profile if kind == "parallel")
    return serial, parallel


def time_on(profile, p):
    serial, parallel = split(profile)
    return serial + parallel / p


def speedup(profile, p):
    serial, parallel = split(profile)
    return (serial + parallel) / time_on(profile, p)


def speedup_limit(profile):
    serial, parallel = split(profile)
    if serial == 0:
        return float("inf")
    return (serial + parallel) / serial


def cores_needed(profile, target):
    serial, parallel = split(profile)
    total = serial + parallel
    t = Fraction(target)
    if t <= 1:
        return 1
    room = total - t * serial
    if room <= 0:
        return None
    return max(1, math.ceil(t * parallel / room))
--- hint
Add up the serial seconds a and the parallel seconds b first. Then Tₚ = a + b / p, T₁ = a + b, and the limit is T₁ / a.
--- hint
For `cores_needed`, a target of 1 or less is met by one core. Otherwise, speedup at least t means p · (T₁ − t·a) ≥ t · b. If T₁ − t·a is 0 or negative, no p works.
--- hint
Otherwise p is the ceiling of t·b / (T₁ − t·a). Make t a `Fraction` so every step is exact, and use `math.ceil`, which works on Fractions. Never loop over p.
--- check test | time_on for the example
abs(time_on([(3, "serial"), (27, "parallel")], 9) - 6.0) < 1e-9
--- check test | One core takes the whole profile
abs(time_on([(2, "serial"), (30, "parallel"), (3, "serial"), (15, "parallel")], 1) - 50.0) < 1e-9
--- check test | Serial phases add up, wherever they are
abs(time_on([(2, "serial"), (30, "parallel"), (3, "serial"), (15, "parallel")], 5) - 14.0) < 1e-9
--- check test | speedup for the example
abs(speedup([(3, "serial"), (27, "parallel")], 9) - 5.0) < 1e-9
--- check test | speedup is 1 on one core
abs(speedup([(4, "serial"), (36, "parallel")], 1) - 1.0) < 1e-9
--- check test | speedup with no serial part is p
abs(speedup([(12, "parallel")], 8) - 8.0) < 1e-9
--- check test | speedup below the limit
abs(speedup([(2, "serial"), (30, "parallel"), (3, "serial"), (15, "parallel")], 5) - 50 / 14) < 1e-9
--- check test | speedup_limit is 1/s
abs(speedup_limit([(2, "serial"), (30, "parallel"), (3, "serial"), (15, "parallel")]) - 10.0) < 1e-9
--- check case | No serial time: no limit
(speedup_limit([(12, "parallel")]), speedup_limit([(0, "serial"), (10, "parallel")]))
=> (float("inf"), float("inf"))
--- check test | All serial: the limit is 1
abs(speedup_limit([(8, "serial")]) - 1.0) < 1e-9
--- check case | cores_needed for the example
cores_needed([(3, "serial"), (27, "parallel")], 5)
=> 9
?? With 9 cores the speedup is exactly 5. Keep the arithmetic exact so it does not round up to 10.
--- check case | Exactly on the target, with four phases
cores_needed([(2, "serial"), (30, "parallel"), (3, "serial"), (15, "parallel")], 5)
=> 9
--- check case | The limit itself is never reached
cores_needed([(2, "serial"), (30, "parallel"), (3, "serial"), (15, "parallel")], 10)
=> None
--- check case | Just under the limit takes many cores
cores_needed([(2, "serial"), (30, "parallel"), (3, "serial"), (15, "parallel")], __import__("fractions").Fraction(999, 100))
=> 8991
--- check case | A target of 1 or less needs one core
(cores_needed([(8, "serial")], 1), cores_needed([(4, "serial"), (36, "parallel")], __import__("fractions").Fraction(1, 2)))
=> (1, 1)
--- check case | All serial: nothing above 1 is possible
cores_needed([(8, "serial")], 2)
=> None
--- check case | No serial part: p = ceiling of the target
(cores_needed([(12, "parallel")], 7), cores_needed([(12, "parallel")], __import__("fractions").Fraction(5, 2)))
=> (7, 3)
--- check case | A fractional target
cores_needed([(4, "serial"), (36, "parallel")], __import__("fractions").Fraction(5, 2))
=> 3
--- check case | 10¹⁸ cores: no loop can count that high
cores_needed([(1, "serial"), (10**9, "parallel")], 10**9)
=> 1000000000000000000
?? Solve p · (T₁ − t·a) ≥ t · b for p with exact arithmetic instead of trying p = 1, 2, 3, …

+++ question | Where the bound comes from
--- ask
A program's serial part takes 5% of its one-core time. Why can it never run more than 20 times faster, on any number of cores?
--- choice
Because adding cores beyond 20 makes the threads fight over memory.
--- choice correct
Because even if the parallel part took no time at all, the serial 5% would still take 1/20 of the original time.
--- choice
Because Amdahl's law assumes at most 20 cores.
--- choice
Because the efficiency drops below 5% after 20 cores.
--- why
Tₚ = T₁ · (s + (1 − s)/p) is always more than s · T₁, so Sₚ = T₁ / Tₚ is always less than 1/s = 20. Memory contention is a real extra cost, but the bound holds even for a perfect machine with none.

+++ question | Amdahl or Gustafson?
--- ask
Amdahl's law says speedup stops below 1/s; Gustafson's law says scaled speedup grows without limit. Which statement is right?
--- choice
Amdahl's, because Gustafson ignores the serial part.
--- choice
Gustafson's, because Amdahl's law was disproved by modern supercomputers.
--- choice correct
Both: Amdahl holds the problem size fixed, while Gustafson lets the problem grow with p, and each measures the serial fraction on a different run.
--- why
Amdahl's s is a fraction of the one-processor time of a fixed job; Gustafson's s′ is a fraction of the p-processor time of a job that grows with p. Converting one into the other with s = s′ / (s′ + p(1 − s′)) gives the same speedup from both formulas.

+++ question | Reading a measurement
--- ask
A job runs 4 times faster on 5 cores than on 1. Using the Karp–Flatt formula s = (1/S − 1/p) / (1 − 1/p), what serial fraction does that imply? Type it as a fraction in lowest terms, like 1/7.
--- answer
1/16
0.0625
--- why
1/S − 1/p = 1/4 − 1/5 = 1/20, and 1 − 1/p = 4/5. So s = (1/20) / (4/5) = 1/16. Check it: 1 / (1/16 + (15/16)/5) = 1 / (1/16 + 3/16) = 4.

+++ practice | Amdahl and Gustafson side by side
--- task
Write three functions. Each takes a serial fraction `s` (a float from 0 to 1) and a number of processors `p` (a whole number, 1 or more), and returns a float.

- `amdahl(s, p)`: the speedup for a fixed-size job whose one-processor time is a fraction `s` serial: 1 / (s + (1 − s) / p).
- `gustafson(s, p)`: the scaled speedup for a job whose `p`-processor run is a fraction `s` serial: s + p · (1 − s).
- `efficiency(speedup, p)`: the speedup divided by `p`.

For example, `amdahl(0.25, 3)` is `2.0`, `gustafson(0.25, 3)` is `2.5`, and `efficiency(2.0, 4)` is `0.5`. When `s` is 0 both laws give `p`; when `s` is 1 both give `1`.
--- starter
def amdahl(s, p):
    return p


def gustafson(s, p):
    return p


def efficiency(speedup, p):
    return 1.0
--- solution
def amdahl(s, p):
    return 1 / (s + (1 - s) / p)


def gustafson(s, p):
    return s + p * (1 - s)


def efficiency(speedup, p):
    return speedup / p
--- hint
Each function is one line: write the formula exactly as the task gives it. Watch the brackets in Amdahl's law: it is (1 − s) that is divided by p.
--- check case | The examples from the task
(round(amdahl(0.25, 3), 9), round(gustafson(0.25, 3), 9), round(efficiency(2.0, 4), 9))
=> (2.0, 2.5, 0.5)
--- check case | No serial part: both give p
(round(amdahl(0.0, 16), 9), round(gustafson(0.0, 16), 9))
=> (16.0, 16.0)
--- check case | All serial: both give 1
(round(amdahl(1.0, 64), 9), round(gustafson(1.0, 64), 9))
=> (1.0, 1.0)
--- check case | One processor: speedup 1 for any s
(round(amdahl(0.3, 1), 9), round(gustafson(0.3, 1), 9))
=> (1.0, 1.0)
--- check case | 5% serial on 64 processors
(round(amdahl(0.05, 64), 4), round(gustafson(0.05, 64), 4))
=> (15.4217, 60.85)
--- check case | Efficiency of Amdahl's speedup falls as p grows
[round(efficiency(amdahl(0.1, p), p), 4) for p in (1, 2, 8, 32)]
=> [1.0, 0.9091, 0.5882, 0.2439]

+++ practice | The serial fraction, measured
--- task
You timed a job on one core (`t1` seconds) and then on several core counts. Amdahl's law, solved for s, gives the **Karp–Flatt metric**: e = (1/S − 1/p) / (1 − 1/p), where S = t1 / tp is the measured speedup on p cores.

Write `karp_flatt(t1, runs)`. `runs` is a list of `(p, tp)` pairs: a core count and the time measured with it. Return a list of `(p, e)` pairs in the same order, with e rounded to 4 decimal places with `round(e, 4)`. The metric means nothing for p = 1 (it divides by zero), so leave out any run with p = 1.

Then write `verdict(pairs)`, which takes the list `karp_flatt` returns and answers `"serial"` if the largest e minus the smallest e is at most 0.005 (the job simply has that much serial work) and `"overhead"` otherwise (something grows with p). Return `"serial"` for an empty list.

For example, `karp_flatt(100, [(2, 60), (4, 40)])` is `[(2, 0.2), (4, 0.2)]`, and its verdict is `"serial"`.
--- starter
def karp_flatt(t1, runs):
    return []


def verdict(pairs):
    return "serial"
--- solution
def karp_flatt(t1, runs):
    out = []
    for p, tp in runs:
        if p == 1:
            continue
        s = t1 / tp
        e = (1 / s - 1 / p) / (1 - 1 / p)
        out.append((p, round(e, 4)))
    return out


def verdict(pairs):
    if not pairs:
        return "serial"
    es = [e for _, e in pairs]
    if max(es) - min(es) <= 0.005:
        return "serial"
    return "overhead"
--- hint
For each run, S = t1 / tp. Then e = (1/S − 1/p) / (1 − 1/p). Skip p = 1 with `continue` before dividing.
--- hint
For `verdict`, collect the e values, and compare `max` minus `min` with 0.005. Handle the empty list first, because `max` of an empty list is an error.
--- check case | The example from the task
(karp_flatt(100, [(2, 60), (4, 40)]), verdict(karp_flatt(100, [(2, 60), (4, 40)])))
=> ([(2, 0.2), (4, 0.2)], "serial")
--- check case | Perfect scaling has no serial part
karp_flatt(64, [(2, 32), (4, 16), (8, 8)])
=> [(2, 0.0), (4, 0.0), (8, 0.0)]
--- check case | p = 1 is left out
karp_flatt(90, [(1, 90), (3, 40)])
=> [(3, 0.1667)]
--- check case | A rising metric means overhead
(karp_flatt(1000, [(2, 520), (4, 290), (8, 190), (16, 150)]), verdict(karp_flatt(1000, [(2, 520), (4, 290), (8, 190), (16, 150)])))
=> ([(2, 0.04), (4, 0.0533), (8, 0.0743), (16, 0.0933)], "overhead")
--- check case | Only p = 1, and nothing at all
(karp_flatt(10, [(1, 10)]), verdict([]))
=> ([], "serial")
--- check case | Small wobble is still serial
verdict([(2, 0.101), (4, 0.1), (8, 0.104)])
=> "serial"

+++ practice | The best number of processors, with overhead
--- task
A job has `a` seconds of serial work and `b` seconds of parallel work on one processor. Using p processors also costs `c` seconds for each processor beyond the first, so the predicted time is

T(p) = a + b/p + c · (p − 1).

Write `best_cores(a, b, c, max_cores)` that returns a pair `(p, T(p))` for the p from 1 to `max_cores` with the smallest time. On a tie, choose the smaller p. All inputs are whole numbers, `a`, `b` and `c` are 0 or more, and `max_cores` is at least 1. Return the time as an exact `Fraction`.

The inputs can be huge (`max_cores` up to 10¹⁸), so trying every p is far too slow. Use the lesson's rule: going from p to p + 1 helps exactly when b / (p(p + 1)) > c, that is when c · p · (p + 1) < b. So the best p is the smallest p with c · p · (p + 1) ≥ b, or `max_cores` if that is smaller. Find it with a binary search, as in DSA I.

For example, `best_cores(10, 400, 1, 100)` is `(20, Fraction(49, 1))`: T(20) = 10 + 20 + 19 = 49.
--- starter
from fractions import Fraction


def best_cores(a, b, c, max_cores):
    return (1, Fraction(a + b))
--- solution
from fractions import Fraction


def time_with(a, b, c, p):
    return a + Fraction(b, p) + c * (p - 1)


def best_cores(a, b, c, max_cores):
    lo, hi = 1, max_cores
    while lo < hi:
        mid = (lo + hi) // 2
        if c * mid * (mid + 1) >= b:
            hi = mid
        else:
            lo = mid + 1
    return (lo, time_with(a, b, c, lo))
--- hint
The test "c · p · (p + 1) ≥ b" is false for small p and true from some point on: a monotone predicate. Binary search for the first p in 1 … max_cores where it is true; if it is never true, the search ends at `max_cores`.
--- hint
Keep `lo = 1` and `hi = max_cores`. While `lo < hi`, test `mid = (lo + hi) // 2`: if the predicate holds, the answer is at most `mid`, so set `hi = mid`; otherwise set `lo = mid + 1`.
--- hint
Compute the time with `a + Fraction(b, p) + c * (p - 1)` so it stays exact. If c is 0 the predicate only holds when b is 0; then every p ties and the search correctly gives 1.
--- check case | The example from the task
best_cores(10, 400, 1, 100)
=> (20, __import__("fractions").Fraction(49, 1))
--- check case | Capped by max_cores
best_cores(10, 400, 1, 8)
=> (8, __import__("fractions").Fraction(67, 1))
--- check case | A tie picks the smaller p
best_cores(0, 12, 2, 10)
=> (2, __import__("fractions").Fraction(8, 1))
?? T(2) = 6 + 2 = 8 and T(3) = 4 + 4 = 8. Stop at the first p where c · p · (p + 1) ≥ b.
--- check case | No overhead: use every processor
best_cores(5, 100, 0, 7)
=> (7, __import__("fractions").Fraction(135, 7))
--- check case | No parallel work: one processor
(best_cores(9, 0, 3, 50), best_cores(9, 0, 0, 50))
=> ((1, __import__("fractions").Fraction(9, 1)), (1, __import__("fractions").Fraction(9, 1)))
--- check case | One processor allowed
best_cores(1, 1000, 1, 1)
=> (1, __import__("fractions").Fraction(1001, 1))
--- check case | Huge inputs
best_cores(7, 10**18, 1, 10**18)
=> (1000000000, __import__("fractions").Fraction(2000000006, 1))
?? Trying every p up to 10¹⁸ never finishes. Binary search needs about 60 steps.

+++ practice | Converting between the two serial fractions
--- task
Amdahl's s is a fraction of the one-processor time. Gustafson's s′ is a fraction of the p-processor time of the same run. They are linked by

s = s′ / (s′ + p · (1 − s′)), and, the other way, s′ = s·p / (s·p + (1 − s)).

Write `to_amdahl(s_scaled, p)` and `to_gustafson(s, p)`. The fractions are given as `Fraction`s or whole numbers (0 or 1) and `p` is a whole number; return an exact `Fraction`. If a fraction is below 0 or above 1, or `p` is below 1, raise `ValueError`.

Your conversions must make the laws agree: `amdahl(to_amdahl(x, p), p)` equals `gustafson(x, p)` exactly, where `amdahl(s, p)` is 1 / (s + (1 − s)/p) and `gustafson(s, p)` is s + p(1 − s). Write those two functions as well, returning exact Fractions when given Fractions.

For example, `to_amdahl(Fraction(1, 2), 3)` is `Fraction(1, 4)`, and both `amdahl(Fraction(1, 4), 3)` and `gustafson(Fraction(1, 2), 3)` are `Fraction(2, 1)`.
--- starter
from fractions import Fraction


def amdahl(s, p):
    return Fraction(p)


def gustafson(s, p):
    return Fraction(p)


def to_amdahl(s_scaled, p):
    return Fraction(s_scaled)


def to_gustafson(s, p):
    return Fraction(s)
--- solution
from fractions import Fraction


def check(frac, p):
    if frac < 0 or frac > 1 or p < 1:
        raise ValueError("fraction must be in [0, 1] and p at least 1")


def amdahl(s, p):
    s = Fraction(s)
    return 1 / (s + (1 - s) / p)


def gustafson(s, p):
    s = Fraction(s)
    return s + p * (1 - s)


def to_amdahl(s_scaled, p):
    check(s_scaled, p)
    s_scaled = Fraction(s_scaled)
    return s_scaled / (s_scaled + p * (1 - s_scaled))


def to_gustafson(s, p):
    check(s, p)
    s = Fraction(s)
    return s * p / (s * p + (1 - s))
--- hint
Turn each input into a `Fraction` first, with `Fraction(x)`, so `1 / (…)` stays exact instead of becoming a float.
--- hint
Check the ranges before any arithmetic and `raise ValueError(...)` when they fail. Can a denominator ever be 0 for valid inputs? s′ + p(1 − s′) is 0 only if s′ = 0 and s′ = 1 at once, so no.
--- check case | The example from the task
(to_amdahl(__import__("fractions").Fraction(1, 2), 3), amdahl(__import__("fractions").Fraction(1, 4), 3), gustafson(__import__("fractions").Fraction(1, 2), 3))
=> (__import__("fractions").Fraction(1, 4), __import__("fractions").Fraction(2, 1), __import__("fractions").Fraction(2, 1))
--- check case | Converting back gives the original
(to_gustafson(__import__("fractions").Fraction(1, 4), 3), to_gustafson(to_amdahl(__import__("fractions").Fraction(1, 20), 64), 64))
=> (__import__("fractions").Fraction(1, 2), __import__("fractions").Fraction(1, 20))
--- check case | The ends: 0 stays 0, 1 stays 1
(to_amdahl(0, 50), to_amdahl(1, 50), to_gustafson(0, 50), to_gustafson(1, 50))
=> (__import__("fractions").Fraction(0, 1), __import__("fractions").Fraction(1, 1), __import__("fractions").Fraction(0, 1), __import__("fractions").Fraction(1, 1))
--- check case | One processor changes nothing
(to_amdahl(__import__("fractions").Fraction(2, 7), 1), to_gustafson(__import__("fractions").Fraction(2, 7), 1))
=> (__import__("fractions").Fraction(2, 7), __import__("fractions").Fraction(2, 7))
--- check test | The two laws agree, for many fractions and processor counts
all(amdahl(to_amdahl(__import__("fractions").Fraction(k, 12), p), p) == gustafson(__import__("fractions").Fraction(k, 12), p) for k in range(13) for p in (1, 2, 5, 64, 1000))
--- check test | Out-of-range inputs raise ValueError
raises(ValueError, lambda: to_amdahl(__import__("fractions").Fraction(3, 2), 4)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: to_gustafson(-1, 4)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: to_amdahl(__import__("fractions").Fraction(1, 2), 0))
--- check test | Results are exact Fractions, not floats
type(to_amdahl(__import__("fractions").Fraction(1, 3), 5)).__name__ == "Fraction" and type(amdahl(__import__("fractions").Fraction(1, 3), 5)).__name__ == "Fraction"

+++ practice | Debug: the prediction that is right only on one core
--- task
`predicted_times(t1, s, cores)` should return, for each core count p in the list `cores`, the time Amdahl's law predicts for a job that takes `t1` seconds on one core and is a fraction `s` serial, rounded to 3 decimal places with `round(..., 3)`.

It gives the right answer for p = 1, but every other prediction is wrong: for `predicted_times(100, 0.2, [1, 4])` it returns `[100.0, 115.0]`, but four cores should take 20 + 80/4 = 40 seconds, so the right answer is `[100.0, 40.0]`. Find the bug and fix it.
--- starter
def predicted_times(t1, s, cores):
    out = []
    for p in cores:
        out.append(round(t1 * (s + 1 - s / p), 3))
    return out
--- solution
def predicted_times(t1, s, cores):
    out = []
    for p in cores:
        out.append(round(t1 * (s + (1 - s) / p), 3))
    return out
--- hint
Write the formula out in words: the serial fraction, plus the parallel fraction divided by p. Which part does the code divide by p?
--- hint
Division binds tighter than subtraction, so `1 - s / p` means 1 − (s/p). The parallel fraction needs its own brackets: `(1 - s) / p`. On one core both happen to equal 1, which is why p = 1 looked right.
--- check case | The example from the task
predicted_times(100, 0.2, [1, 4])
=> [100.0, 40.0]
--- check case | Several core counts
predicted_times(60, 0.25, [2, 3, 15])
=> [37.5, 30.0, 18.0]
--- check case | No serial part divides perfectly
predicted_times(64, 0.0, [1, 2, 8])
=> [64.0, 32.0, 8.0]
--- check case | All serial never gets faster
predicted_times(9, 1.0, [1, 100])
=> [9.0, 9.0]
--- check case | An empty list of core counts

+++ practice | Sharing cores among pipeline stages
--- task
A ground station processes every telemetry frame through a pipeline of stages, one after another: decode, correct, calibrate, store. Stage i has `serial` seconds of serial work and `parallel` seconds of parallel work per frame, so with c cores it takes serial + parallel / c seconds. The frame's total time is the sum over all stages.

You have `k` cores to share out, and every stage must get at least 1. Write `share_cores(stages, k)` where `stages` is a list of `(serial, parallel)` pairs of whole numbers. Return a list with the number of cores for each stage, in stage order, that makes the total time as small as possible.

Use this greedy rule: start with 1 core per stage, then hand out the remaining cores one at a time, each to the stage whose time would drop the most. Giving stage i its (c + 1)-th core saves parallel / c − parallel / (c + 1) = parallel / (c(c + 1)) seconds. Compare savings **exactly** (with `Fraction`, or by cross-multiplying whole numbers); on a tie, give the core to the stage with the smaller index. The greedy rule is optimal here because each stage's savings shrink as it gets more cores.

`k` is at least the number of stages. It can be large: a check shares 60,000 cores among 3,000 stages, so find the best stage with a heap (`heapq`), not by scanning every stage for every core.

For example, `share_cores([(1, 12), (2, 4)], 4)` is `[3, 1]`. There are two spare cores. The first goes to stage 0 (saving 12/2 = 6, against 4/2 = 2 for stage 1). The second also goes to stage 0: its next saving, 12/6 = 2, ties with stage 1's 2, and stage 0 has the smaller index.
--- starter
def share_cores(stages, k):
    cores = [1] * len(stages)
    cores[0] += k - len(stages)
    return cores
--- solution
import heapq
from fractions import Fraction


def share_cores(stages, k):
    cores = [1] * len(stages)
    heap = []
    for i, (serial, parallel) in enumerate(stages):
        heapq.heappush(heap, (-Fraction(parallel, 2), i))
    for _ in range(k - len(stages)):
        _, i = heapq.heappop(heap)
        cores[i] += 1
        c = cores[i]
        heapq.heappush(heap, (-Fraction(stages[i][1], c * (c + 1)), i))
    return cores
--- hint
Keep a heap of `(-saving, index)` pairs, one per stage: `heapq` pops the smallest item, so negating the saving pops the biggest saving, and the index breaks ties towards the smaller stage.
--- hint
A stage's first saving (from 1 core to 2) is parallel / 2. Each time you pop a stage, add a core to it and push its next saving, parallel / (c(c + 1)) with its new count c. Use `Fraction(parallel, c * (c + 1))` so ties compare exactly.
--- check case | The example from the task
share_cores([(1, 12), (2, 4)], 4)
=> [3, 1]
--- check case | Exactly one core each
share_cores([(5, 100), (1, 1), (0, 50)], 3)
=> [1, 1, 1]
--- check case | Stages with no parallel work get nothing extra
share_cores([(9, 0), (1, 30), (4, 0)], 6)
=> [1, 4, 1]
--- check case | Ties all the way go to the smaller index
share_cores([(0, 6), (0, 6), (0, 6)], 5)
=> [2, 2, 1]
--- check case | A bigger mix
share_cores([(2, 90), (0, 10), (3, 40), (1, 160)], 12)
=> [4, 1, 2, 5]
--- check test | Matches the best allocation found by trying them all
(lambda F, st, k: (lambda best: sum(F(s) + F(b, c) for (s, b), c in zip(st, share_cores(st, k))) == best)(min(sum(F(s) + F(b, c) for (s, b), c in zip(st, (x, y, z))) for x in range(1, k) for y in range(1, k) for z in range(1, k) if x + y + z == k)))(__import__("fractions").Fraction, [(1, 35), (4, 72), (0, 18)], 11)
--- check case | 60,000 cores among 3,000 stages
(lambda cs: (len(cs), sum(cs), min(cs), cs[:8]))(share_cores([((i * 7) % 5, (i * 7919) % 1000) for i in range(3000)], 60000))
=> (3000, 60000, 1, [1, 29, 27, 26, 25, 23, 22, 20])
?? Scanning 3,000 stages for each of 57,000 cores is 171 million comparisons. A heap finds the best stage in about 12 steps.
=== par-02 | Work and span: a parallel program as a graph
--- teach
Last lesson measured parallel programs from the outside, with a single number: the serial fraction. Real programs are not one serial part and one perfectly divisible part. They are many pieces of work, some of which must wait for others. This lesson models a parallel computation as a graph of tasks and gives it two numbers, **work** and **span**. From those two numbers alone you can say how fast any number of processors can possibly run it, and prove that a simple scheduler gets within a factor of 2 of that.

### A computation as a graph

Picture a kitchen preparing one dish. Chopping the onions and boiling the water can happen at the same time, but frying the onions must wait for the chopping. Draw each job as a dot and each "must wait for" as an arrow, and you have a map of what can run in parallel.

That map is a **directed acyclic graph** (DAG), from DSA I: a set of nodes joined by arrows, with no cycle. Here:

- each **node** is a task: a piece of sequential work with a **cost**, the time it takes on one processor;
- each **edge** u → v means v cannot start until u has finished: a **dependency**.

The DAG has no cycles because a cycle would mean a task waiting for itself. Here is a telemetry job, with each task's cost in brackets:

```text
            load (2)
           /        \
   decode A (3)   decode B (1)
          |           |
   filter A (4)   filter B (1)
           \        /
            merge (2)
```

Decoding A and decoding B can run at the same time; so can the two filters. Merge must wait for both filters.

### Work and span

Two numbers describe the whole DAG.

- **Work**, written **T₁**: the sum of all the costs. It is the time one processor needs, doing the tasks one after another in any order that respects the arrows. Here T₁ = 2 + 3 + 1 + 4 + 1 + 2 = 13.
- **Span**, written **T∞**: the total cost of the most expensive path through the DAG, following the arrows. It is the time the job would take with unlimited processors, because the tasks on a path must still run one after another. Here the heaviest path is load, decode A, filter A, merge: 2 + 3 + 4 + 2 = 11. That path is the **[[critical path|critical-path]]**.

Their ratio **T₁ / T∞** is the **parallelism**: the average amount of work available per step along the critical path. Here it is 13 / 11 ≈ 1.18. However many processors this job gets, it can never run more than 1.18 times faster.

### Two lower bounds

Let Tₚ be the time on p processors, with any schedule at all. Two laws hold.

- **The work law: Tₚ ≥ T₁ / p.** In each unit of time, p processors do at most p units of work. All T₁ units must be done, so it takes at least T₁ / p time.
- **The span law: Tₚ ≥ T∞.** The tasks on the critical path must run one after another, each starting only when the one before it has finished. Having more processors cannot overlap them.

Together they bound the speedup: **T₁ / Tₚ ≤ min(p, T₁ / T∞)**. Speedup is at most the number of processors, and at most the parallelism. The span law is Amdahl's law in a more precise form: instead of guessing a serial fraction, the graph shows exactly which chain of work cannot be sped up.

### Getting the span from the graph

Computing the work is a sum. Computing the span means finding the heaviest path in a DAG, which the recursion "the heaviest path ending at v is v's cost plus the heaviest path ending at any of its predecessors" solves. Call that value **finish(v)**: the earliest time v can finish with unlimited processors.

- A task with no predecessors has finish(v) = cost(v).
- Otherwise, finish(v) = cost(v) + the largest finish(u) over the predecessors u of v.
- The span is the largest finish(v) over all v.

The recursion is only usable in an order where every predecessor is handled before the tasks after it: a **topological order**, which Kahn's algorithm from DSA I gives. For the telemetry job:

| task | predecessors | finish |
|---|---|---|
| load | none | 2 |
| decode A | load | 2 + 3 = 5 |
| decode B | load | 2 + 1 = 3 |
| filter A | decode A | 5 + 4 = 9 |
| filter B | decode B | 3 + 1 = 4 |
| merge | filter A, filter B | max(9, 4) + 2 = 11 |

Each node and each edge is handled once, so this takes **Θ(V + E)** time for V nodes and E edges.

### Scheduling unit tasks

Now the scheduler. To keep the argument clean, give every task cost 1, so time goes in **steps**. A task is **ready** when all its predecessors have finished in earlier steps. In each step, a scheduler picks up to p ready tasks and runs them.

A **greedy scheduler** never leaves a processor idle when there is a ready task: in each step it runs min(p, number of ready tasks) tasks. Here is one on 8 unit tasks with p = 2, choosing the ready tasks with the smallest numbers:

```text
0   1          edges: 0→2  0→3  1→3  1→4
|\ /|                 2→5  3→5  3→6  4→6
| X |                 5→7  6→7
2 3 4
 \|\|
  5 6
   \|
    7
```

| step | ready at the start | runs |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | 0, 1 | 0, 1 |
| 2 | 2, 3, 4 | 2, 3 |
| 3 | 4, 5 | 4, 5 |
| 4 | 6 | 6 |
| 5 | 7 | 7 |

Steps 1 to 3 are **complete steps**: all p processors were busy. Steps 4 and 5 are **incomplete steps**: fewer than p tasks were ready, so every ready task ran. Here T₁ = 8 and T∞ = 4 (for example 0 → 3 → 5 → 7), so the lower bounds say at least max(8/2, 4) = 4 steps. The schedule took 5, and no schedule can do better here: to finish in 4 steps, tasks 5 and 6 would have to run in step 3, so 2, 3 and 4 would all have to run in step 2, which is three tasks on two processors.

Keeping the ready tasks in a heap (from DSA I) lets you take the smallest ones quickly:

```python
import heapq

ready = [4, 9, 2]
heapq.heapify(ready)
batch = [heapq.heappop(ready) for _ in range(min(2, len(ready)))]
print(batch, ready)      # [2, 4] [9]
```

### Why greedy is nearly optimal

**Theorem ([[Brent|brent]], and Graham).** Every greedy schedule of unit tasks on p processors takes at most **⌊T₁ / p⌋ + T∞** steps.

Count the two kinds of steps separately.

1. **Complete steps.** Each one runs p tasks, and there are only T₁ tasks in all. So there are at most ⌊T₁ / p⌋ complete steps.
2. **Incomplete steps.** Look at the tasks that have not run yet, and take a longest path among them. Its first task has no unfinished predecessor: if it had one, you could put that predecessor in front and get a longer path. So the first task of every longest remaining path is ready. An incomplete step runs **every** ready task, so it removes the first task of every longest remaining path, and the longest remaining path gets 1 shorter. It started at T∞, and it cannot go below 0, so there are at most T∞ incomplete steps.

Adding the two gives the bound. Now compare it with the best possible schedule, whose time T* is at least both T₁ / p and T∞:

⌊T₁ / p⌋ + T∞ ≤ T* + T* = 2 · T*.

So **any greedy scheduler is within a factor of 2 of optimal**, on every DAG, without knowing anything about the future. Finding a truly optimal schedule is NP-hard in general (DSA II's last lessons), so this is the guarantee real schedulers settle for.

The bound says more. The speedup of a greedy schedule is at least T₁ / (T₁/p + T∞) = p / (1 + p / P), where P = T₁ / T∞ is the parallelism. When P is much bigger than p, say P ≥ 10 · p, the speedup is at least p / 1.1: nearly linear. This is called [[parallel slackness|slackness]], and it is the design rule of parallel programming: **make the parallelism much larger than the number of processors, and a simple scheduler does the rest.** For tasks of different costs, the same argument (by Ron Graham, 1966) gives Tₚ ≤ T₁/p + (1 − 1/p) · T∞.

### Fork and join

Most parallel programs build their DAGs in a regular way, with **[[fork-join|fork-join]]** parallelism: a task **forks** (spawns) subtasks that may run in parallel, and later **joins** (waits for) them all. Two rules give the work and span of any fork-join program:

- **In sequence** (A, then B): work adds, and span adds. W = W_A + W_B, S = S_A + S_B.
- **In parallel** (A and B at once, then join): work adds, but span takes the larger. W = W_A + W_B, S = max(S_A, S_B).

Summing an array by splitting it in half, summing both halves in parallel, and adding the two results gives the recurrences

W(n) = 2 · W(n/2) + 1 and S(n) = S(n/2) + 1,

since the two halves run in parallel and the addition comes after. So W(n) = Θ(n), like a loop, but S(n) = Θ(log n): parallelism n / log n, which for a million numbers is about 50,000. Plenty.

Now merge sort from DSA I, with the two halves sorted in parallel but the merge done by one processor:

W(n) = 2 · W(n/2) + Θ(n) = Θ(n log n), but S(n) = S(n/2) + Θ(n) = Θ(n),

because the top-level merge alone costs n. The parallelism is only Θ(log n): about 20 for a million items, however many processors you have. The merge is the bottleneck, and lesson 7 shows how to merge in parallel.

In C++ the same pattern can be written with `std::async`, which runs a function on another thread and hands back a **future** that you can wait on (lesson 6 covers futures properly):

```cpp fragment
long long sum(const std::vector<int>& v, std::size_t lo, std::size_t hi) {
    if (hi - lo <= 10000) return std::accumulate(v.begin() + lo, v.begin() + hi, 0LL);
    std::size_t mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;
    auto left = std::async(std::launch::async, sum, std::cref(v), lo, mid);   // fork
    long long right = sum(v, mid, hi);
    return left.get() + right;                                                // join
}
```

### Where it is used

- **Task-parallel runtimes** such as Cilk, Intel oneTBB, Java's ForkJoinPool and Rust's Rayon are built around work and span. Cilk's tools report both numbers for a real program, so you can see the parallelism before buying cores.
- **Build systems.** `make -j 8` runs a DAG of compile and link steps on 8 workers. The link step waits for every compile, so the span is the slowest compile plus the link.
- **Project planning.** The critical path method schedules construction and spacecraft integration the same way, and says which tasks cannot slip without delaying launch.
- **Data pipelines.** Tools like Apache Spark and Airflow turn a job into a DAG of stages and schedule them greedily across a cluster.

**Watch out:**

- **The span is the heaviest path, not the longest one.** Count costs, not tasks. A path of two tasks costing 10 each is heavier than a path of five tasks costing 1.
- **A topological order is required.** Computing finish(v) in plain index order uses predecessors that have not been computed yet, unless every edge happens to go from a lower index to a higher one.
- **Parallelism is not speedup.** T₁ / T∞ is an upper bound on speedup. You reach it only with enough processors and a good scheduler.
- **Fork-join spans take the max, not the sum.** Two parallel branches cost max(S_A, S_B) in span, but W_A + W_B in work.

::: context critical-path The path that sets the pace
The critical path method was developed in the late 1950s at DuPont and Remington Rand to plan chemical-plant maintenance; the US Navy's PERT method for the Polaris missile programme appeared at the same time. Every task on the critical path has zero slack: delaying it by a day delays the whole project by a day. Tasks off the path can slip a little without harm. Shortening a task off the critical path does not speed the project up at all, which is exactly the span law.
:::

::: context brent The bound behind every scheduler
Richard Brent proved a version of this bound in 1974 for evaluating arithmetic expressions in parallel. Ron Graham had proved the list-scheduling bound for tasks of any length in 1966, while working on scheduling problems at Bell Labs. The version with work and span is the one taught today, thanks to the Cilk project at MIT in the 1990s, which built a real language and scheduler around it and proved that its randomised scheduler meets the bound in expectation, as lesson 6 describes.
:::

::: context slackness More parallelism than processors
Slackness is the ratio P / p: how many times more parallelism the program has than the machine has processors. With P / p = 1 the bound only promises about half of linear speedup; with P / p = 10 it promises over 90%. So programmers deliberately create far more tasks than cores, for example splitting a loop of a million iterations into thousands of pieces for eight cores. The cost of making too many tiny tasks is overhead, which lesson 6 weighs against slackness.
:::

::: context fork-join Spawn, then wait
In the picture, the parent forks two children, which may run in parallel, and joins them before continuing. The span follows the heavier child; the work counts both.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 300 170" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">

  <text x="150" y="24" font-size="10" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">fork</text>
  <rect x="40" y="60" width="80" height="40" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1"/>
  <text x="80" y="84" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">A: span 5</text>

  <text x="220" y="84" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">B: span 2</text>
  <circle cx="150" cy="140" r="12" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1"/>
  <text x="150" y="144" font-size="10" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">join</text>

  <line x1="158" y1="29" x2="215" y2="70" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <line x1="85" y1="100" x2="142" y2="131" stroke="#1f2a44"/>

  <text x="150" y="166" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#b4232c">span max(5, 2) = 5, work 5 + 2 = 7</text>
</svg>
```
:::
--- task
A DAG has tasks numbered 0 to n − 1. An edge `(u, v)` means task v cannot start until task u has finished. Edges may go from a higher number to a lower one.

Write two functions:

- `work_span(costs, edges)`: `costs[i]` is task i's cost, a whole number 1 or more, and n is `len(costs)` (at least 1). Return the pair `(work, span)`: the sum of the costs, and the cost of the heaviest path. It must run in O(n + E) time: a check uses 200,000 tasks.
- `greedy_steps(n, edges, p)`: every task costs 1. Simulate the greedy scheduler on `p` processors: in each step, the ready tasks are those whose predecessors all ran in earlier steps; run the `p` ready tasks with the smallest numbers (or all of them, if fewer are ready). Return the list of steps, each step a sorted list of the tasks it ran. A check uses 100,000 tasks, so keep the ready tasks in a heap.

For example, for costs `[4, 1, 2]` and edges `[(1, 0), (1, 2)]`, `work_span` gives `(7, 5)`: the heaviest path is 1 → 0. For n = 4, edges `[(0, 3), (1, 3), (2, 3)]` and p = 2, `greedy_steps` gives `[[0, 1], [2], [3]]`.
--- starter
def work_span(costs, edges):
    return (sum(costs), max(costs))


def greedy_steps(n, edges, p):
    return [[i] for i in range(n)]
--- solution
import heapq


def graph(n, edges):
    succ = [[] for _ in range(n)]
    indeg = [0] * n
    for u, v in edges:
        succ[u].append(v)
        indeg[v] += 1
    return succ, indeg


def work_span(costs, edges):
    n = len(costs)
    succ, indeg = graph(n, edges)
    finish = list(costs)
    queue = [v for v in range(n) if indeg[v] == 0]
    for u in queue:
        for v in succ[u]:
            finish[v] = max(finish[v], finish[u] + costs[v])
            indeg[v] -= 1
            if indeg[v] == 0:
                queue.append(v)
    return (sum(costs), max(finish))


def greedy_steps(n, edges, p):
    succ, indeg = graph(n, edges)
    ready = [v for v in range(n) if indeg[v] == 0]
    heapq.heapify(ready)
    steps = []
    while ready:
        batch = [heapq.heappop(ready) for _ in range(min(p, len(ready)))]
        for u in batch:
            for v in succ[u]:
                indeg[v] -= 1
                if indeg[v] == 0:
                    heapq.heappush(ready, v)
        steps.append(batch)
    return steps
--- hint
Build a list of successors and a count of unfinished predecessors (the in-degree) for every task. Kahn's algorithm from DSA I then visits the tasks in a topological order: start with the tasks of in-degree 0, and when a task is done, lower each successor's count, adding it once the count reaches 0.
--- hint
For the span, start `finish` as a copy of `costs`. When you handle an edge u → v in Kahn's order, set `finish[v]` to the larger of itself and `finish[u] + costs[v]`. The span is the largest value in `finish`.
--- hint
For the schedule, keep the ready tasks in a heap. Each step pops min(p, len(ready)) tasks, then lowers the counts of their successors and pushes the ones that reach 0. Push them only after the whole batch is chosen, so a task never runs in the same step as its predecessor.
--- check case | work_span for the example
work_span([4, 1, 2], [(1, 0), (1, 2)])
=> (7, 5)
--- check case | A single task
work_span([9], [])
=> (9, 9)
--- check case | No edges: the span is the biggest task
work_span([3, 8, 2, 5], [])
=> (18, 8)
--- check case | The heaviest path is not the one with most tasks
work_span([10, 10, 1, 1, 1, 1, 1], [(0, 1), (2, 3), (3, 4), (4, 5), (5, 6)])
=> (25, 20)
--- check case | A diamond with a heavy side
work_span([2, 3, 1, 4, 1, 2], [(0, 1), (0, 2), (1, 3), (2, 4), (3, 5), (4, 5)])
=> (13, 11)
--- check case | Edges from higher numbers to lower ones
work_span([1, 2, 3, 4], [(3, 2), (2, 1), (1, 0)])
=> (10, 10)
?? Handle tasks in a topological order, not in number order.
--- check case | Two paths into one task: the heavier wins
work_span([5, 1, 1, 1, 7], [(1, 2), (2, 3), (3, 4), (0, 4)])
=> (15, 12)
--- check test | 200,000 tasks in a chain that runs backwards
work_span([1] * 200000, [(i + 1, i) for i in range(199999)]) == (200000, 200000)
--- check case | greedy_steps for the example
greedy_steps(4, [(0, 3), (1, 3), (2, 3)], 2)
=> [[0, 1], [2], [3]]
--- check case | The eight-task DAG from the lesson, with p = 2 and p = 3
(greedy_steps(8, [(0, 2), (0, 3), (1, 3), (1, 4), (2, 5), (3, 5), (3, 6), (4, 6), (5, 7), (6, 7)], 2), greedy_steps(8, [(0, 2), (0, 3), (1, 3), (1, 4), (2, 5), (3, 5), (3, 6), (4, 6), (5, 7), (6, 7)], 3))
=> ([[0, 1], [2, 3], [4, 5], [6], [7]], [[0, 1], [2, 3, 4], [5, 6], [7]])
--- check case | One processor runs tasks one at a time, smallest ready first
greedy_steps(4, [(3, 0)], 1)
=> [[1], [2], [3], [0]]
--- check case | A task never runs in the same step as its predecessor
greedy_steps(3, [(0, 1), (1, 2)], 5)
=> [[0], [1], [2]]
?? Choose the whole step's tasks before releasing their successors.
--- check case | More processors than tasks
greedy_steps(3, [], 10)
=> [[0, 1, 2]]
--- check test | The steps obey the dependencies and the greedy bound
(lambda n, es, p: (lambda st: sorted(t for s in st for t in s) == list(range(n)) and all(len(s) <= p for s in st) and all({t: k for k, s in enumerate(st) for t in s}[u] < {t: k for k, s in enumerate(st) for t in s}[v] for u, v in es) and len(st) <= n // p + work_span([1] * n, es)[1])(greedy_steps(n, es, p)))(60, [(i, j) for i in range(60) for j in range(i + 1, 60) if (i * 31 + j * 17) % 11 == 0], 3)
--- check case | 100,000 tasks: a wide tree
(lambda st: (len(st), st[0], st[1], st[2], st[-1]))(greedy_steps(100000, [((i - 1) // 4, i) for i in range(1, 100000)], 4))
=> (25001, [0], [1, 2, 3, 4], [5, 6, 7, 8], [99997, 99998, 99999])

+++ question | The step in the greedy proof
--- ask
The proof that a greedy schedule takes at most ⌊T₁/p⌋ + T∞ steps says every incomplete step shortens the longest remaining path. Why is the first task of a longest remaining path always ready?
--- choice
Because the greedy scheduler always picks tasks on the critical path first.
--- choice correct
Because if it had an unfinished predecessor, putting that predecessor in front would give a longer path, which is impossible.
--- choice
Because tasks with smaller numbers become ready first.
--- choice
Because every task is ready after T∞ steps.
--- why
A greedy scheduler knows nothing about paths; it just runs every ready task in an incomplete step. The argument works because a longest path cannot be extended backwards, so its first task has no unfinished predecessor and is ready, and so it runs.

+++ question | Merge sort's parallelism
--- ask
Merge sort sorts its two halves in parallel but merges them on one processor. For n items, what is its parallelism, T₁ / T∞?
--- choice
Θ(n), because each level of recursion can run in parallel.
--- choice
Θ(n / log n), like the parallel sum.
--- choice correct
Θ(log n), because the span S(n) = S(n/2) + Θ(n) is Θ(n) while the work is Θ(n log n).
--- choice
Θ(1), because merging is sequential.
--- why
The top-level merge alone costs Θ(n), so the span is at least that: S(n) = n + n/2 + n/4 + … = Θ(n). Dividing the work Θ(n log n) by Θ(n) leaves Θ(log n), about 20 for a million items.

+++ question | The greedy bound in numbers
--- ask
A computation has work 1,000 and span 10, all tasks of cost 1. What is the most steps any greedy scheduler can take on 4 processors? Type a whole number.
--- answer
260
--- why
⌊T₁/p⌋ + T∞ = ⌊1000/4⌋ + 10 = 250 + 10 = 260. The lower bounds say no schedule beats max(250, 10) = 250, so greedy is within 4% of optimal here: the parallelism of 100 is 25 times the processor count.

+++ practice | Work and span of a fork-join program
--- task
A fork-join program is written as a nested structure:

- a whole number is one task with that cost;
- `("seq", [a, b, …])` runs its parts one after another;
- `("par", [a, b, …])` runs its parts at the same time, then joins them.

Write `fork_join(prog)` that returns the pair `(work, span)`. In sequence, work adds and span adds. In parallel, work adds but span is the largest part's span. An empty list of parts has work 0 and span 0.

For example, `fork_join(("seq", [2, ("par", [3, 5]), 1]))` is `(11, 8)`: the work is 2 + 3 + 5 + 1, and the span is 2 + max(3, 5) + 1.
--- starter
def fork_join(prog):
    return (0, 0)
--- solution
def fork_join(prog):
    if isinstance(prog, int):
        return (prog, prog)
    kind, parts = prog
    results = [fork_join(part) for part in parts]
    work = sum(w for w, _ in results)
    if kind == "seq":
        span = sum(s for _, s in results)
    else:
        span = max((s for _, s in results), default=0)
    return (work, span)
--- hint
Recursion: a whole number is its own work and span. For a `("seq", parts)` or `("par", parts)` pair, first work out every part, then combine.
--- hint
Work is always the sum of the parts' work. Span is the sum for `"seq"` and the maximum for `"par"`; `max(..., default=0)` handles an empty list of parts.
--- check case | The example from the task
fork_join(("seq", [2, ("par", [3, 5]), 1]))
=> (11, 8)
--- check case | A single task
fork_join(7)
=> (7, 7)
--- check case | Parallel parts: work adds, span is the biggest
fork_join(("par", [4, 9, 2]))
=> (15, 9)
--- check case | Nested forks
fork_join(("par", [("seq", [1, ("par", [6, 2]), 1]), ("seq", [3, 3, 3])]))
=> (19, 9)
--- check case | Empty parts
(fork_join(("seq", [])), fork_join(("par", [])), fork_join(("seq", [("par", []), 4])))
=> ((0, 0), (0, 0), (4, 4))
--- check case | A balanced tree of additions
fork_join(("seq", [("par", [("seq", [("par", [1, 1]), 1]), ("seq", [("par", [1, 1]), 1])]), 1]))
=> (7, 3)

+++ practice | Work and span from the recurrences
--- task
Divide-and-conquer programs give recurrences for their work and span. Write two functions, each returning the pair `(work, span)` for an input of size n (a whole number, 1 or more). For n ≥ 2 the input splits into a part of size ⌊n/2⌋ and a part of size ⌈n/2⌉, and both parts run in parallel.

- `parallel_sum(n)`: W(1) = 1 and S(1) = 1. For n ≥ 2, the two parts, then one addition: W(n) = W(⌊n/2⌋) + W(⌈n/2⌉) + 1 and S(n) = max(S(⌊n/2⌋), S(⌈n/2⌉)) + 1.
- `serial_merge_sort(n)`: W(1) = 1 and S(1) = 1. For n ≥ 2, the two parts, then a merge on one processor that costs n: W(n) = W(⌊n/2⌋) + W(⌈n/2⌉) + n and S(n) = max(S(⌊n/2⌋), S(⌈n/2⌉)) + n.

n can be as large as 10¹⁸. Written naively the recursion makes about n calls, but at each depth only two different sizes appear (⌊n/2ᵏ⌋ and ⌈n/2ᵏ⌉), so remember results you have already computed, for example with `functools.cache`.

For example, `parallel_sum(4)` is `(7, 3)` and `serial_merge_sort(4)` is `(12, 7)`.
--- starter
def parallel_sum(n):
    return (n, n)


def serial_merge_sort(n):
    return (n, n)
--- solution
from functools import cache


@cache
def parallel_sum(n):
    if n == 1:
        return (1, 1)
    w1, s1 = parallel_sum(n // 2)
    w2, s2 = parallel_sum(n - n // 2)
    return (w1 + w2 + 1, max(s1, s2) + 1)


@cache
def serial_merge_sort(n):
    if n == 1:
        return (1, 1)
    w1, s1 = serial_merge_sort(n // 2)
    w2, s2 = serial_merge_sort(n - n // 2)
    return (w1 + w2 + n, max(s1, s2) + n)
--- hint
⌊n/2⌋ is `n // 2`, and ⌈n/2⌉ is `n - n // 2`. Each function calls itself on both sizes and combines the results by the rule in the task.
--- hint
Put `@cache` (from `functools`) on the line above each `def`. Then each size is computed once, and 10¹⁸ needs only about 120 calls.
--- check case | The examples from the task
(parallel_sum(4), serial_merge_sort(4))
=> ((7, 3), (12, 7))
--- check case | Size 1
(parallel_sum(1), serial_merge_sort(1))
=> ((1, 1), (1, 1))
--- check case | Odd sizes split unevenly
(parallel_sum(5), serial_merge_sort(5))
=> ((9, 4), (17, 11))
--- check case | A million items
(parallel_sum(10**6), serial_merge_sort(10**6))
=> ((1999999, 21), (20951424, 2000007))
--- check case | 10¹⁸ items
(parallel_sum(10**18), serial_merge_sort(10**18))
=> ((1999999999999999999, 61), (60847078495393153024, 2000000000000000018))
?? Without remembering results, 10¹⁸ takes about 10¹⁸ calls. With @cache, about 120.
--- check test | The sum's span is logarithmic; merge sort's span is linear
parallel_sum(2**40)[1] == 41 and serial_merge_sort(2**40)[1] == 2**41 - 1

+++ practice | The critical path and the slack
--- task
A launch campaign is a DAG of tasks numbered 0 to n − 1, with `costs[i]` days each (whole numbers, 1 or more) and edges `(u, v)` meaning v starts only after u finishes. With unlimited crews, the campaign takes span days.

For each task, the **earliest start** is the largest finish time among its predecessors (0 if it has none). The **latest start** is the latest day it can start without delaying the end: for a task with no successors it is span − cost, and otherwise it is (the smallest latest start among its successors) − its own cost. The **slack** is latest start − earliest start. Tasks with slack 0 are **critical**.

Write `critical_tasks(costs, edges)` that returns the pair `(span, critical)`, where `critical` is the sorted list of tasks with slack 0. Edges may go from higher numbers to lower. It must run in O(n + E): a check uses 100,000 tasks.

For example, for costs `[3, 2, 4, 1]` and edges `[(0, 1), (0, 2), (1, 3), (2, 3)]`, the span is 3 + 4 + 1 = 8, and tasks 0, 2 and 3 are critical; task 1 has slack 2.
--- starter
def critical_tasks(costs, edges):
    return (sum(costs), list(range(len(costs))))
--- solution
def critical_tasks(costs, edges):
    n = len(costs)
    succ = [[] for _ in range(n)]
    indeg = [0] * n
    for u, v in edges:
        succ[u].append(v)
        indeg[v] += 1
    order = [v for v in range(n) if indeg[v] == 0]
    for u in order:
        for v in succ[u]:
            indeg[v] -= 1
            if indeg[v] == 0:
                order.append(v)
    earliest = [0] * n
    for u in order:
        for v in succ[u]:
            earliest[v] = max(earliest[v], earliest[u] + costs[u])
    span = max(earliest[v] + costs[v] for v in range(n))
    latest = [0] * n
    for u in reversed(order):
        if succ[u]:
            latest[u] = min(latest[v] for v in succ[u]) - costs[u]
        else:
            latest[u] = span - costs[u]
    critical = [v for v in range(n) if latest[v] == earliest[v]]
    return (span, critical)
--- hint
First find a topological order with Kahn's algorithm. Earliest starts go forwards through it: for each edge u → v, the earliest start of v is at least earliest[u] + costs[u].
--- hint
The span is the largest earliest[v] + costs[v]. Latest starts go **backwards** through the same order, so every successor is done before the task itself.
--- hint
A task is critical when its latest start equals its earliest start. Every critical task lies on some heaviest path.
--- check case | The example from the task
critical_tasks([3, 2, 4, 1], [(0, 1), (0, 2), (1, 3), (2, 3)])
=> (8, [0, 2, 3])
--- check case | One task
critical_tasks([5], [])
=> (5, [0])
--- check case | Independent tasks: only the longest is critical
critical_tasks([2, 7, 7, 1], [])
=> (7, [1, 2])
--- check case | Two equally heavy paths are both critical
critical_tasks([1, 3, 3, 2], [(0, 1), (0, 2), (1, 3), (2, 3)])
=> (6, [0, 1, 2, 3])
--- check case | Edges from higher to lower numbers
critical_tasks([2, 1, 5, 3], [(3, 2), (2, 0), (3, 1), (1, 0)])
=> (10, [0, 2, 3])
--- check case | A long cheap chain against one heavy task
critical_tasks([1, 1, 1, 1, 10, 2], [(0, 1), (1, 2), (2, 3), (3, 5), (4, 5)])
=> (12, [4, 5])
--- check test | 100,000 tasks: two chains, the heavier one critical
(lambda r: r[0] == 100000 and r[1] == list(range(0, 100000, 2)))(critical_tasks([2 if i % 2 == 0 else 1 for i in range(100000)], [(i, i + 2) for i in range(99998)]))

+++ practice | Bounds on the running time
--- task
From work and span alone you can bound the time on p processors when every task costs 1. No schedule is faster than the **lower bound** max(⌈work / p⌉, span), and every greedy schedule is at least as fast as the **greedy bound** ⌊work / p⌋ + span.

Write `time_bounds(work, span, p)` that returns the pair `(lower, greedy)` as whole numbers. The inputs are whole numbers. Raise `ValueError` if p < 1, if span < 0, if span > work (a path cannot cost more than everything), or if span is 0 while work is more than 0 (a task lies on some path). Work 0 and span 0 give `(0, 0)`.

Then write `verdict(work, span, p, t)`: `"impossible"` if t is below the lower bound, `"guaranteed"` if t is at least the greedy bound, and `"possible"` otherwise.

Work can be huge, so use whole-number arithmetic only: `work / p` is a float and loses digits above about 2⁵³.

For example, `time_bounds(1000, 10, 4)` is `(250, 260)`, and `verdict(1000, 10, 4, 255)` is `"possible"`.
--- starter
def time_bounds(work, span, p):
    return (max(work / p, span), work / p + span)


def verdict(work, span, p, t):
    return "possible"
--- solution
def time_bounds(work, span, p):
    if p < 1 or span < 0 or span > work or (span == 0 and work > 0):
        raise ValueError("impossible work, span or p")
    lower = max(-(-work // p), span)
    greedy = work // p + span
    return (lower, greedy)


def verdict(work, span, p, t):
    lower, greedy = time_bounds(work, span, p)
    if t < lower:
        return "impossible"
    if t >= greedy:
        return "guaranteed"
    return "possible"
--- hint
⌈a / b⌉ for whole numbers is `-(-a // b)`, and ⌊a / b⌋ is `a // b`. Neither ever makes a float.
--- hint
Check the four bad cases first and raise before computing anything. In `verdict`, call `time_bounds`, then compare t with each bound.
--- check case | The example from the task
(time_bounds(1000, 10, 4), verdict(1000, 10, 4, 255))
=> ((250, 260), "possible")
--- check case | Work that does not divide evenly
time_bounds(10, 2, 4)
=> (3, 4)
--- check case | One processor: both bounds are the work, or more
time_bounds(12, 5, 1)
=> (12, 17)
--- check case | A chain: span equals work
time_bounds(9, 9, 3)
=> (9, 12)
--- check case | More processors than tasks
time_bounds(5, 2, 100)
=> (2, 2)
--- check case | Nothing to do
time_bounds(0, 0, 8)
=> (0, 0)
--- check case | Verdicts at the edges
(verdict(100, 5, 10, 9), verdict(100, 5, 10, 10), verdict(100, 5, 10, 14), verdict(100, 5, 10, 15))
=> ("impossible", "possible", "possible", "guaranteed")
--- check test | Bad inputs raise ValueError
raises(ValueError, lambda: time_bounds(10, 2, 0)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: time_bounds(5, 6, 2)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: time_bounds(5, 0, 2)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: time_bounds(5, -1, 2))
--- check case | Huge work, exactly
time_bounds(10**18 + 3, 7, 2)
=> (500000000000000002, 500000000000000008)
?? A float cannot hold 10¹⁸ + 3 exactly. Use // for floor and -(-a // b) for ceiling.

+++ practice | Debug: the rounds that start too early
--- task
A **level-synchronous** executor runs a DAG in rounds: round 0 runs every task with no predecessors, and each task runs in the round after the **last** of its predecessors. So a task's round is the number of tasks on the longest path ending just before it. `rounds(n, edges)` should return the list of rounds, each a sorted list of tasks.

It works on trees, but on some DAGs a task is put in a round before one of its predecessors has run. For example, with n = 4 and edges `[(0, 1), (1, 2), (0, 3), (2, 3)]`, task 3 needs task 2, which runs in round 2, so task 3 belongs in round 3. The code returns `[[0], [1, 3], [2]]` instead of `[[0], [1], [2], [3]]`. Find the bug and fix it.
--- starter
def rounds(n, edges):
    succ = [[] for _ in range(n)]
    indeg = [0] * n
    for u, v in edges:
        succ[u].append(v)
        indeg[v] += 1
    level = [0] * n
    seen = [False] * n
    order = [v for v in range(n) if indeg[v] == 0]
    for u in order:
        for v in succ[u]:
            if not seen[v]:
                seen[v] = True
                level[v] = level[u] + 1
            indeg[v] -= 1
            if indeg[v] == 0:
                order.append(v)
    out = [[] for _ in range(max(level, default=-1) + 1)]
    for v in range(n):
        out[level[v]].append(v)
    return out
--- solution
def rounds(n, edges):
    succ = [[] for _ in range(n)]
    indeg = [0] * n
    for u, v in edges:
        succ[u].append(v)
        indeg[v] += 1
    level = [0] * n
    order = [v for v in range(n) if indeg[v] == 0]
    for u in order:
        for v in succ[u]:
            level[v] = max(level[v], level[u] + 1)
            indeg[v] -= 1
            if indeg[v] == 0:
                order.append(v)
    out = [[] for _ in range(max(level, default=-1) + 1)]
    for v in range(n):
        out[level[v]].append(v)
    return out
--- hint
The code sets a task's round from the **first** predecessor that reaches it, the way breadth-first search sets distances. What should decide the round instead?
--- hint
The round must come after every predecessor's round: take the maximum over all of them, `level[v] = max(level[v], level[u] + 1)`, every time an edge is handled. The `seen` list is then not needed.
--- check case | The example from the task
rounds(4, [(0, 1), (1, 2), (0, 3), (2, 3)])
=> [[0], [1], [2], [3]]
--- check case | A tree was already right
rounds(5, [(0, 1), (0, 2), (1, 3), (1, 4)])
=> [[0], [1, 2], [3, 4]]
--- check case | A shortcut edge does not pull a task earlier
rounds(5, [(4, 0), (0, 1), (1, 2), (4, 2), (2, 3)])
=> [[4], [0], [1], [2], [3]]
--- check case | No edges, and no tasks
(rounds(3, []), rounds(0, []))
=> ([[0, 1, 2]], [])
--- check case | Two sources joining late
rounds(6, [(0, 2), (2, 4), (1, 3), (3, 4), (1, 5), (4, 5)])
=> [[0, 1], [2, 3], [4], [5]]

+++ practice | make -j: scheduling tasks of different lengths
--- task
A build has tasks numbered 0 to n − 1 with `costs[i]` seconds each (whole numbers, 1 or more) and edges `(u, v)` meaning v starts only after u finishes. Simulate a greedy **list scheduler** on `p` workers, like `make -j p`:

- Time starts at 0 with every worker free. Whenever a worker is free and a task is ready (all its predecessors finished), the worker starts a task immediately.
- When several tasks are ready, start the one with the largest **bottom level** first: its cost plus the heaviest path from it to the end of the DAG (so the bottom level of a task with no successors is its own cost). Break ties by the smaller task number.
- When several workers are free, it does not matter which one takes the task. Tasks that finish at time t release their successors at time t, before anything is started at time t.

Write `list_schedule(costs, edges, p)` that returns the pair `(makespan, starts)`: the time the last task finishes, and a list with each task's start time.

For example, for costs `[2, 3, 1, 1]`, edges `[(0, 3)]` and p = 2: the bottom levels are 3, 3, 1 and 1. At time 0, tasks 0 and 1 tie at 3 and both start. Task 0 finishes at 2, which releases task 3; tasks 2 and 3 tie at 1, so task 2 starts at 2. Task 1 finishes at 3 and task 3 starts at 3, finishing at 4. The answer is `(4, [0, 0, 2, 3])`.

Graham's bound promises makespan ≤ work / p + (1 − 1/p) · span; a check tests it.
--- starter
def list_schedule(costs, edges, p):
    return (sum(costs), [0] * len(costs))
--- solution
import heapq


def list_schedule(costs, edges, p):
    n = len(costs)
    succ = [[] for _ in range(n)]
    indeg = [0] * n
    for u, v in edges:
        succ[u].append(v)
        indeg[v] += 1
    order = [v for v in range(n) if indeg[v] == 0]
    left = list(indeg)
    for u in order:
        for v in succ[u]:
            left[v] -= 1
            if left[v] == 0:
                order.append(v)
    bottom = list(costs)
    for u in reversed(order):
        for v in succ[u]:
            bottom[u] = max(bottom[u], costs[u] + bottom[v])
    ready = [(-bottom[v], v) for v in range(n) if indeg[v] == 0]
    heapq.heapify(ready)
    running = []
    starts = [0] * n
    free = p
    now = 0
    makespan = 0
    while ready or running:
        while ready and free > 0:
            _, v = heapq.heappop(ready)
            starts[v] = now
            heapq.heappush(running, (now + costs[v], v))
            free -= 1
        now = running[0][0]
        while running and running[0][0] == now:
            _, u = heapq.heappop(running)
            free += 1
            makespan = max(makespan, now)
            for v in succ[u]:
                indeg[v] -= 1
                if indeg[v] == 0:
                    heapq.heappush(ready, (-bottom[v], v))
    return (makespan, starts)
--- hint
Compute the bottom levels first, going **backwards** through a topological order: bottom[u] is costs[u] plus the largest bottom level among u's successors.
--- hint
Simulate with two heaps: ready tasks keyed by `(-bottom, task)`, so the largest bottom level and then the smallest number comes out first, and running tasks keyed by `(finish time, task)`. Start ready tasks while workers are free, then jump the clock to the earliest finish time.
--- hint
At each finish time, pop **every** task finishing then, free their workers and release their successors, and only then start new tasks. The makespan is the last finish time.
--- check case | The example from the task
list_schedule([2, 3, 1, 1], [(0, 3)], 2)
=> (4, [0, 0, 2, 3])
--- check case | One worker runs everything back to back
list_schedule([3, 1, 2], [], 1)
=> (6, [0, 5, 3])
?? Bottom levels 3, 1 and 2: task 0 first, then task 2, then task 1.
--- check case | A chain cannot go faster than its span
list_schedule([2, 2, 2], [(0, 1), (1, 2)], 4)
=> (6, [0, 2, 4])
--- check case | The critical task goes first
list_schedule([1, 1, 1, 5], [(0, 3)], 2)
=> (6, [0, 0, 1, 1])
--- check case | Tasks finishing together release their successors together
list_schedule([2, 2, 3, 1], [(0, 2), (1, 2), (0, 3)], 2)
=> (5, [0, 0, 2, 2])
--- check test | Graham's bound holds on a bigger build (work 190, span 30), and dependencies are respected
(lambda costs, es: (lambda r: r[0] * 3 <= 190 + 2 * 30 and r[0] >= 30 and all(r[1][v] >= r[1][u] + costs[u] for u, v in es))(list_schedule(costs, es, 3)))([(i * 37) % 9 + 1 for i in range(40)], [(i, j) for i in range(40) for j in range(i + 1, 40) if (i * 13 + j * 7) % 10 == 0])
--- check case | A bigger build, exactly
list_schedule([(i * 37) % 9 + 1 for i in range(12)], [(0, 5), (1, 5), (2, 6), (5, 9), (6, 9), (3, 10), (9, 11), (10, 11), (4, 7), (7, 8)], 3)
=> (22, [2, 0, 0, 9, 0, 3, 3, 5, 13, 10, 13, 15])
