=== par-03 | Parallel patterns: map, reduce, scan and stencil
--- teach
Last lesson gave every parallel computation two numbers, work and span, and showed that a greedy scheduler turns a small span into real speedup. This lesson is about the handful of shapes that most parallel programs are built from. Learn these four **patterns**, with their work and span, and you can see the parallel structure in a new problem at a glance. The star is the **scan**: it looks hopelessly sequential, and yet it can be done in logarithmic span with no extra work.

To reason about them precisely, picture an idealised machine: in each **round**, any number of processors each do one operation, all reading the values from before the round and writing new values at its end. Real machines are messier, but the counts carry over.

### Map

A **map** applies the same function to every item, independently: brightness correction on every pixel, unit conversion on every reading. No item's result depends on another's, so all of them can run at once.

- Work: n applications of the function. Span: one application.

A map is **[[embarrassingly parallel|embarrassing]]**: the only limits are how fast you can hand out the items and collect the results. In C++17 and later, the standard algorithms take an **execution policy**; `std::execution::par` allows the library to split the work across threads:

```cpp fragment
std::transform(std::execution::par, raw.begin(), raw.end(), volts.begin(),
               [](int counts) { return counts * 0.0025; });
```

### Reduce

A **reduce** combines all items into one with a binary operator ⊕: a sum, a maximum, a product, a count. Done one by one, a reduce has span n − 1. Done as a **tree**, it is much shorter: in round 1, pair the items up and combine each pair; in round 2, combine the pairs' results; and so on. Each round halves the number of values.

```text
round 0:   4   9   2   7   5   1   8   3
round 1:    13      9       6      11
round 2:        22             17
round 3:               39
```

- Work: n − 1 combinations, the same as the loop. Span: ⌈log₂ n⌉ rounds.

The tree regroups the operations: it computes ((4 ⊕ 9) ⊕ (2 ⊕ 7)) ⊕ …, where the loop computes (((4 ⊕ 9) ⊕ 2) ⊕ 7) ⊕ …. These are equal only if ⊕ is **[[associative|associative]]**: (a ⊕ b) ⊕ c = a ⊕ (b ⊕ c) for all a, b, c. Associativity is exactly what makes any grouping give the same answer, as long as the items stay in their order. Addition, multiplication, max, min and string concatenation are associative. Subtraction is not: (8 − 3) − 2 = 3, but 8 − (3 − 2) = 7.

Notice what the tree does **not** need: **commutativity**, a ⊕ b = b ⊕ a. The tree always puts the left item on the left. String concatenation is associative but not commutative, and a tree reduce of `["ab", "c", "de"]` still gives `"abcde"`. Python's `functools.reduce` does the loop version:

```python
from functools import reduce

print(reduce(lambda a, b: a + b, ["ab", "c", "de"]))      # abcde
print(reduce(max, [4, 9, 2, 7]))                         # 9
```

A reduce also needs an **identity**: a value e with e ⊕ a = a ⊕ e = a, such as 0 for addition, 1 for multiplication and "" for concatenation. It is the answer for an empty list, and it can pad a list to a convenient length without changing the result.

### Scan

A **scan**, or **prefix sum**, produces every running total. For items x₀, x₁, …, xₙ₋₁:

- the **inclusive scan** is x₀, x₀ ⊕ x₁, x₀ ⊕ x₁ ⊕ x₂, …: position i holds everything up to and including xᵢ;
- the **exclusive scan** is e, x₀, x₀ ⊕ x₁, …: position i holds everything **before** xᵢ, and starts with the identity.

For fuel burned per minute `[3, 1, 7, 0, 4]`, the inclusive scan is `[3, 4, 11, 11, 15]` (fuel used by the end of each minute), and the exclusive scan is `[0, 3, 4, 11, 11]` (fuel used before each minute starts). Python's `itertools.accumulate` gives the inclusive scan, one step at a time:

```python
from itertools import accumulate

print(list(accumulate([3, 1, 7, 0, 4])))                  # [3, 4, 11, 11, 15]
```

The loop has span n − 1, because each total needs the one before. It looks as if a scan cannot be parallel at all. It can, in two ways.

### Hillis and Steele: few rounds, extra work

In round k = 0, 1, 2, …, let the offset be d = 2ᵏ. Every position i ≥ d replaces its value with (its left neighbour at distance d, from before the round) ⊕ (its own value from before the round). Positions i < d keep their value. Here is an inclusive sum scan of `[2, 5, 1, 4, 3]`:

| after round | offset d | array |
|---|---|---|
| start | | 2, 5, 1, 4, 3 |
| 0 | 1 | 2, 7, 6, 5, 7 |
| 1 | 2 | 2, 7, 8, 12, 13 |
| 2 | 4 | 2, 7, 8, 12, 15 |

**Why it works.** The invariant: after the round with offset d, position i holds the combination of the items from max(0, i − 2d + 1) to i, the last 2d items ending at i. Before the round, position i covered the last d items ending at i, and position i − d covered the d items just before those. Combining them, left first, covers 2d items in a row. Positions below d already covered everything from 0 and keep it. After ⌈log₂ n⌉ rounds, 2d ≥ n, so every position covers everything from 0: the inclusive scan.

**Cost.** Span ⌈log₂ n⌉ rounds, but each round does up to n operations, so the work is Θ(n log n). A loop does n − 1. For a million items that is 20 times the work: on a machine with fewer than 20 processors, Hillis–Steele is slower than the loop. An algorithm whose work is more than a constant times the best sequential algorithm's is not **work-efficient**.

### Blelloch: logarithmic span, linear work

Guy Blelloch's scan (1990) is work-efficient. It computes the exclusive scan in place, on a list whose length m is a power of two (pad a shorter list with the identity). It has two sweeps over an imaginary balanced tree whose leaves are the items.

**Up-sweep** (a tree reduce, done in place). For d = 1, 2, 4, …, m/2: for every i = 2d − 1, 4d − 1, 6d − 1, … below m, set a[i] = a[i − d] ⊕ a[i]. All the updates for one d are independent, so each d is one round.

**Down-sweep.** Set a[m − 1] to the identity. Then for d = m/2, m/4, …, 1: for every i = 2d − 1, 4d − 1, … below m, let t be the old a[i − d]; set a[i − d] to the old a[i], and a[i] to (old a[i]) ⊕ t.

Here it is on `[3, 1, 7, 0, 4, 1, 6, 3]`, with sums:

| step | array |
|---|---|
| start | 3, 1, 7, 0, 4, 1, 6, 3 |
| up, d = 1 | 3, **4**, 7, **7**, 4, **5**, 6, **9** |
| up, d = 2 | 3, 4, 7, **11**, 4, 5, 6, **14** |
| up, d = 4 | 3, 4, 7, 11, 4, 5, 6, **25** |
| clear the last | 3, 4, 7, 11, 4, 5, 6, **0** |
| down, d = 4 | 3, 4, 7, **0**, 4, 5, 6, **11** |
| down, d = 2 | 3, **0**, 7, **4**, 4, **11**, 6, **16** |
| down, d = 1 | **0**, **3**, **4**, **11**, **11**, **15**, **16**, **22** |

The result is the exclusive scan: 0, 3, 3 + 1, 3 + 1 + 7, and so on.

**Why it works.** Think of the positions in blocks of size 2d: the block **ending** at i covers positions i − 2d + 1 to i.

1. **After the up-sweep**, for each d, the position at the end of each block of size 2d holds that block's total. By induction on d: combining the totals of its two halves, left half first, gives the total of the block. (The last position ends up with the total of everything.)
2. **In the down-sweep**, the invariant is: when level d starts, the position at the end of each block of size 2d holds the combination of **everything before that block**, its exclusive prefix. At the start (d = m/2) there is one block, the whole list, with nothing before it, and a[m − 1] was just set to the identity.
3. The block splits into a left half, ending at i − d, and a right half, ending at i. The left half has the same prefix as the whole block, so it gets the old a[i]. The right half's prefix is the block's prefix followed by the left half's total, and the left half's total is exactly what the up-sweep left at i − d. So a[i] becomes (old a[i]) ⊕ t, prefix first, preserving the order. The invariant holds for the halves.
4. At the end, the blocks are single items, so each position holds everything before it: the exclusive scan.

**Cost.** The up-sweep does m/2 + m/4 + … + 1 = m − 1 operations, and so does the down-sweep: **work 2(m − 1) = Θ(n)**, about twice the loop. There are log₂ m rounds in each sweep: **span 2 log₂ m = Θ(log n)**. The parallelism is Θ(n / log n).

### Scan on a real multicore

With p processors and n much bigger than p, neither round-by-round algorithm is what runs. Instead:

1. split the list into p blocks, and let each processor scan its own block with a loop;
2. scan the p block totals (tiny, so one processor is fine);
3. each processor adds the total of all blocks before its own to every item in its block.

The work is about 2n and the span about 2n/p + p. That is what `std::inclusive_scan(std::execution::par, …)` and GPU libraries do, with the block totals scanned by Blelloch's algorithm when p is large.

### What scans are for

A scan turns "where does my output go?" into a parallel question. **Stream compaction** keeps only the items that pass a test, in order. Keep the readings above 50 in `[12, 70, 55, 8, 91]`:

1. **map** each item to a flag, 1 if it passes: `[0, 1, 1, 0, 1]`;
2. **exclusive scan** the flags: `[0, 0, 1, 2, 2]`. For a kept item, this is how many kept items come before it: its position in the output;
3. **scatter**: every kept item writes itself to its position, all at once. The output has (last scan value + last flag) = 3 items: `[70, 55, 91]`.

Every step is a map or a scan, so the whole filter has span Θ(log n). The same trick splits a list into two groups, which is the core of parallel radix sort, and assigns output space when each item produces a different number of results.

### Stencil

A **stencil** updates every cell of a grid from its neighbours, over and over: new[i] depends on old[i − 1], old[i] and old[i + 1]. Heat spreading along a rod, blurring an image, and weather models are all stencils. Every cell of one step can be computed in parallel, but the steps must run in order.

- Work: n cells × s steps. Span: s steps.

The rule "every cell reads the **old** values" is essential, so real code keeps two arrays and swaps them after each step: **double buffering**. Updating one array in place makes cell i read a neighbour that was already updated this step, and the result depends on the order the cells were visited, which in a parallel run is unpredictable. Here is the difference on a rod `[0, 0, 90, 0, 0]`, where each inner cell becomes the average of itself and its two neighbours (ends fixed):

| | cell 1 | cell 2 | cell 3 |
|---|---|---|---|
| double-buffered | (0 + 0 + 90) / 3 = 30 | (0 + 90 + 0) / 3 = 30 | (90 + 0 + 0) / 3 = 30 |
| in place, left to right | 30 | (30 + 90 + 0) / 3 = 40 | (40 + 0 + 0) / 3 ≈ 13.3 |

With p processors, each owns one block of cells and keeps a copy of its neighbours' edge cells, called [[halo cells|halo]], which are exchanged after every step.

### Where it is used

- **Every parallel library** offers these patterns by name: C++'s `std::transform`, `std::reduce`, `std::inclusive_scan` and `std::exclusive_scan`; MPI's `MPI_Reduce` and `MPI_Scan` on clusters; `thrust` and CUB on NVIDIA [[GPUs|gpu]].
- **MapReduce and Spark** (lesson 12) are map and reduce, scaled to thousands of machines.
- **Scans** appear inside radix sort, stream compaction, sparse-matrix code, text layout and parsing.
- **Stencils** run weather and climate models, fluid simulations, image filters and the thermal analysis of a spacecraft.

**Watch out:**

- **Reading values updated in the same round.** Hillis–Steele and stencils must read the values from before the round. Updating in place gives wrong answers that depend on the visiting order.
- **Operators that are not associative.** A tree reduce or scan of subtraction gives a different answer from the loop. And [[floating-point addition|float-assoc]] is not exactly associative, so a parallel sum of floats can differ from the serial sum in the last digits, and from run to run.
- **Inclusive against exclusive.** Mixing them up shifts every result by one place. The exclusive scan starts with the identity; the inclusive scan ends with the total.
- **Reordering.** `std::reduce` may regroup **and reorder** the items, so it needs a commutative operator too; `std::accumulate` goes left to right. For concatenation, use a reduce that keeps the order.

::: context embarrassing Parallel with no effort
Embarrassingly parallel means that splitting the problem takes no cleverness at all: the pieces are independent. Rendering the frames of an animated film, checking millions of passwords against a hash, running a Monte Carlo simulation with different random seeds, and processing each telemetry frame on its own are all embarrassingly parallel. The name is a joke among researchers: such problems are too easy to publish about. In practice they are where parallel computing pays off most reliably.
:::

::: context associative Why any grouping works
Associativity is stated for three items, but it implies that every way of putting brackets around a sequence of any length gives the same result. The proof is by strong induction on the length: any bracketing splits the sequence into a left part and a right part at its top-level operator. By induction each part equals its left-to-right fold, and associativity lets you move the split point one item at a time until it reaches the far left, giving the plain left-to-right fold. So a tree, a loop and a blocked scan all agree.
:::

::: context halo Copies of the neighbours' edges
A worker computing cells 1000 to 1999 of a rod needs cell 999 and cell 2000 to update its end cells, but those belong to its neighbours. So it keeps one extra cell at each end, a halo or ghost cell, and after every step it swaps edge values with its neighbours. The computation grows with the block's size, but the exchange only with its edges: for a square block of a 2D grid of side k, k² cells compute and 4k are exchanged. Bigger blocks mean less communication per unit of work.
:::

::: context gpu Thousands of simple cores
A graphics processing unit has thousands of simple cores that run the same instruction on different data, in groups of 32 or 64. That suits maps, reductions, scans and stencils perfectly, which is why GPUs now train neural networks and run physics simulations as well as drawing images. The Blelloch scan and its relatives were refined for GPUs in the 2000s; NVIDIA's CUB library scans billions of items per second with a single pass over memory.
:::

::: context float-assoc Rounding makes grouping matter
A float keeps about 16 significant decimal digits, and every addition rounds. So `(0.1 + 0.2) + 0.3` is `0.6000000000000001` in Python, while `0.1 + (0.2 + 0.3)` is `0.6`. Worse, `(1e16 + 1.0) - 1e16` is `0.0` while `1e16 - 1e16 + 1.0` is `1.0`. A parallel reduction groups the additions differently from a loop, and the grouping can change with the number of threads, so results can differ between runs. Scientific codes that need identical results fix the grouping, or add in higher precision.
:::
--- task
Implement Blelloch's work-efficient scan. An operator `op(a, b)` is associative but **not** necessarily commutative, so always keep the left item on the left. `identity` is its identity.

- `upsweep(a, op)`: `a` is a list whose length m is a power of two (1 or more). Change it in place: for d = 1, 2, 4, …, m/2, for every i = 2d − 1, 4d − 1, … below m, set `a[i] = op(a[i - d], a[i])`. Return nothing.
- `downsweep(a, op, identity)`: `a` is a list that `upsweep` has just changed. Set the last item to `identity`, then for d = m/2, m/4, …, 1, for every i = 2d − 1, 4d − 1, … below m: remember `t = a[i - d]`, set `a[i - d] = a[i]`, then set `a[i] = op(a[i], t)` using the **old** `a[i]`. Return nothing.
- `exclusive_scan(xs, op, identity)`: return a **new** list, the exclusive scan of `xs` (position i holds the combination of everything before position i, and position 0 holds `identity`). Copy `xs`, pad the copy with `identity` up to the next power of two, run both sweeps, and return the first `len(xs)` items. Do not change `xs`. An empty list gives `[]`.

A check counts the calls to `op`: a scan of 1,000 items (padded to 1,024) must use at most 2 · 1,023 = 2,046.

For example, with `op = lambda a, b: a + b` and identity 0, `exclusive_scan([5, 2, 8], op, 0)` is `[0, 5, 7]`, and with string concatenation and identity `""`, `exclusive_scan(["x", "y", "z"], op, "")` is `["", "x", "xy"]`.
--- starter
def upsweep(a, op):
    pass


def downsweep(a, op, identity):
    pass


def exclusive_scan(xs, op, identity):
    return [identity] * len(xs)
--- solution
def upsweep(a, op):
    m = len(a)
    d = 1
    while d < m:
        for i in range(2 * d - 1, m, 2 * d):
            a[i] = op(a[i - d], a[i])
        d *= 2


def downsweep(a, op, identity):
    m = len(a)
    a[m - 1] = identity
    d = m // 2
    while d >= 1:
        for i in range(2 * d - 1, m, 2 * d):
            t = a[i - d]
            a[i - d] = a[i]
            a[i] = op(a[i], t)
        d //= 2


def exclusive_scan(xs, op, identity):
    n = len(xs)
    if n == 0:
        return []
    m = 1
    while m < n:
        m *= 2
    a = list(xs) + [identity] * (m - n)
    upsweep(a, op)
    downsweep(a, op, identity)
    return a[:n]
--- hint
In both sweeps, the positions for one value of d are `range(2 * d - 1, m, 2 * d)`. The up-sweep's d goes 1, 2, 4, … while d < m; the down-sweep's d goes m // 2, m // 4, … down to 1.
--- hint
In the down-sweep, save `t = a[i - d]` before overwriting it. The new right value is `op(a[i], t)`: the prefix (the old `a[i]`) on the left, the left half's total on the right. Swapping the order breaks non-commutative operators.
--- hint
In `exclusive_scan`, find the smallest power of two m ≥ n by doubling from 1, pad a copy with `[identity] * (m - n)`, run both sweeps on the copy, and slice off the first n items.
--- check test | upsweep leaves block totals at the ends of blocks
(lambda a: (upsweep(a, lambda x, y: x + y), a)[1] == [2, 7, 4, 17, 1, 7, 3, 32])([2, 5, 4, 6, 1, 6, 3, 5])
--- check test | upsweep keeps the order with concatenation
(lambda a: (upsweep(a, lambda x, y: x + y), a)[1] == ["a", "ab", "c", "abcd"])(["a", "b", "c", "d"])
--- check test | downsweep finishes an exclusive scan
(lambda a: (downsweep(a, lambda x, y: x + y, 0), a)[1] == [0, 2, 7, 11, 17, 18, 24, 27])([2, 7, 4, 17, 1, 7, 3, 32])
--- check test | downsweep with concatenation
(lambda a: (downsweep(a, lambda x, y: x + y, ""), a)[1] == ["", "a", "ab", "abc"])(["a", "ab", "c", "abcd"])
?? The new right value is op(old a[i], t): the prefix goes on the left.
--- check test | The sweeps work on a list of one item
(lambda a: (upsweep(a, lambda x, y: x + y), downsweep(a, lambda x, y: x + y, 0), a)[2] == [0])([42])
--- check case | exclusive_scan, the examples from the task
(exclusive_scan([5, 2, 8], lambda a, b: a + b, 0), exclusive_scan(["x", "y", "z"], lambda a, b: a + b, ""))
=> ([0, 5, 7], ["", "x", "xy"])
--- check case | An empty list and a single item
(exclusive_scan([], lambda a, b: a + b, 0), exclusive_scan([9], lambda a, b: a + b, 0))
=> ([], [0])
--- check case | Maximum, with identity minus infinity
exclusive_scan([3, 9, 2, 11, 5, 4], max, float("-inf"))
=> [float("-inf"), 3, 9, 9, 11, 11]
--- check case | Concatenation over a length that is not a power of two
exclusive_scan(["p", "q", "r", "s", "t", "u"], lambda a, b: a + b, "")
=> ["", "p", "pq", "pqr", "pqrs", "pqrst"]
--- check test | 2 × 2 matrix products keep their order
(lambda mul, ms: exclusive_scan(ms, mul, ((1, 0), (0, 1))) == [((1, 0), (0, 1)), ms[0], mul(ms[0], ms[1]), mul(mul(ms[0], ms[1]), ms[2])])(lambda A, B: tuple(tuple(sum(A[i][k] * B[k][j] for k in range(2)) for j in range(2)) for i in range(2)), [((1, 1), (0, 1)), ((2, 0), (1, 1)), ((0, 1), (1, 0)), ((3, 1), (1, 1))])
--- check test | Does not change xs
(lambda xs: (exclusive_scan(xs, lambda a, b: a + b, 0), xs)[1] == [4, 4, 4])([4, 4, 4])
--- check test | 1,000 items: correct, with at most 2,046 calls to op
(lambda box: (lambda out: out == [i * (i - 1) // 2 for i in range(1000)] and len(box) <= 2046)(exclusive_scan(list(range(1000)), lambda a, b: box.append(1) or a + b, 0)))([])
?? Hillis–Steele would use about 9,000 calls here. The up-sweep and down-sweep use m − 1 each.
--- check test | upsweep really builds the tree: 16 items use exactly 15 calls
(lambda box, a: (upsweep(a, lambda x, y: box.append(1) or x + y), len(box) == 15 and a[15] == 136 and a[7] == 36 and a[11] == 42)[1])([], list(range(1, 17)))

+++ question | Hillis–Steele or Blelloch?
--- ask
Hillis–Steele's scan takes about log₂ n rounds and Blelloch's about 2 log₂ n. Why is Blelloch's usually preferred for large n?
--- choice
Because Blelloch's works for non-associative operators.
--- choice correct
Because Hillis–Steele does Θ(n log n) work against Blelloch's Θ(n), so with fewer processors than about log n it is slower than even a loop.
--- choice
Because Hillis–Steele gives the inclusive scan, which is less useful.
--- choice
Because Blelloch's algorithm needs no identity element.
--- why
Every round of Hillis–Steele updates nearly every position, so the total work is about n log₂ n. Real machines have far fewer processors than items, and then the time is about work / p, so doing log n times more work than necessary costs log n times more time.

+++ question | What std::reduce needs
--- ask
You want the sum of a vector of `double` and you call `std::reduce(std::execution::par, v.begin(), v.end(), 0.0)`. Which is true?
--- choice
The result is always bit-for-bit equal to a left-to-right loop, since addition is associative.
--- choice correct
The result may differ slightly from a loop, and even between runs, because floating-point addition is not exactly associative and the library may group the additions differently.
--- choice
It will not compile, because `double` addition is not associative.
--- why
`std::reduce` is allowed to regroup and reorder the items in any way, which is only guaranteed to give the loop's answer for operators that are both associative and commutative. Floating-point addition is commutative but only approximately associative, so the rounding, and therefore the last digits, can change with the grouping.

+++ question | Counting Blelloch's work
--- ask
How many operator calls does Blelloch's scan (up-sweep plus down-sweep) make on a list of 16 items? Type a whole number.
--- answer
30
--- why
The up-sweep does 8 + 4 + 2 + 1 = 15 calls and the down-sweep another 15: 2(m − 1) = 30. A loop would do 15, and Hillis–Steele 15 + 14 + 12 + 8 = 49.

+++ practice | A tree reduce, round by round
--- task
Write `tree_reduce(xs, op)` that reduces a non-empty list with an associative operator `op` as a tree, in rounds. In each round, combine neighbouring pairs, left item on the left: positions 0 and 1, positions 2 and 3, and so on. If the round starts with an odd number of values, the last one is carried into the next round unchanged. Stop when one value is left.

Return the pair `(result, rounds)`. A single item needs 0 rounds. Raise `ValueError` for an empty list.

For example, `tree_reduce([1, 2, 3, 4, 5], lambda a, b: a + b)` is `(15, 3)`: the rounds give `[3, 7, 5]`, then `[10, 5]`, then `[15]`.
--- starter
def tree_reduce(xs, op):
    total = xs[0]
    for x in xs[1:]:
        total = op(total, x)
    return (total, len(xs) - 1)
--- solution
def tree_reduce(xs, op):
    if not xs:
        raise ValueError("cannot reduce an empty list")
    values = list(xs)
    rounds = 0
    while len(values) > 1:
        nxt = [op(values[i], values[i + 1]) for i in range(0, len(values) - 1, 2)]
        if len(values) % 2 == 1:
            nxt.append(values[-1])
        values = nxt
        rounds += 1
    return (values[0], rounds)
--- hint
Keep a list of the current values. One round builds a new list from `op(values[i], values[i + 1])` for i = 0, 2, 4, …, then adds the last value if the count was odd.
--- hint
Repeat while more than one value is left, counting rounds. The number of rounds for n items is ⌈log₂ n⌉.
--- check case | The example from the task
tree_reduce([1, 2, 3, 4, 5], lambda a, b: a + b)
=> (15, 3)
--- check case | One item: no rounds
tree_reduce(["only"], lambda a, b: a + b)
=> ("only", 0)
--- check case | Concatenation keeps the order
tree_reduce(["t", "e", "l", "e", "m", "e", "t", "r", "y"], lambda a, b: a + b)
=> ("telemetry", 4)
--- check case | Rounds for a power of two, and just past it
(tree_reduce(list(range(16)), max)[1], tree_reduce(list(range(17)), max)[1])
=> (4, 5)
--- check test | The pairing is exactly as described
tree_reduce(list("abcde"), lambda a, b: "(" + a + b + ")")[0] == "(((ab)(cd))e)"
--- check test | An empty list raises ValueError
raises(ValueError, lambda: tree_reduce([], max))

+++ practice | The Hillis–Steele scan
--- task
Write `hillis_steele(xs, op)` that computes the **inclusive** scan of `xs` with an associative operator `op` by the Hillis–Steele method, and returns the list of arrays after each round (not including the starting array).

In the round with offset d (d = 1, 2, 4, … while d < n), every position i ≥ d becomes `op(old[i - d], old[i])`, where `old` is the array from **before** the round, and positions i < d keep their value. The last array in the list you return is the inclusive scan. A list of 0 or 1 items needs no rounds, so return `[]`. Do not change `xs`.

For example, `hillis_steele([1, 1, 1, 1], lambda a, b: a + b)` is `[[1, 2, 2, 2], [1, 2, 3, 4]]`.
--- starter
def hillis_steele(xs, op):
    a = list(xs)
    rounds = []
    d = 1
    while d < len(a):
        for i in range(d, len(a)):
            a[i] = op(a[i - d], a[i])
        rounds.append(list(a))
        d *= 2
    return rounds
--- solution
def hillis_steele(xs, op):
    a = list(xs)
    rounds = []
    d = 1
    while d < len(a):
        a = [a[i] if i < d else op(a[i - d], a[i]) for i in range(len(a))]
        rounds.append(a)
        d *= 2
    return rounds
--- hint
The starter updates the list in place, so position i can read a position i − d that was already changed in this round. Every read in a round must see the array from before the round.
--- hint
Build each round's array as a brand-new list from the old one, for example with a comprehension over all positions, then replace the old list with it.
--- check case | The example from the task
hillis_steele([1, 1, 1, 1], lambda a, b: a + b)
=> [[1, 2, 2, 2], [1, 2, 3, 4]]
--- check case | Five items take three rounds
hillis_steele([4, 0, 3, 1, 2], lambda a, b: a + b)
=> [[4, 4, 3, 4, 3], [4, 4, 7, 8, 6], [4, 4, 7, 8, 10]]
--- check case | Empty and single-item lists
(hillis_steele([], max), hillis_steele([7], max))
=> ([], [])
--- check case | Concatenation keeps the order
hillis_steele(["a", "b", "c"], lambda x, y: x + y)
=> [["a", "ab", "bc"], ["a", "ab", "abc"]]
--- check test | Running maximum of 100 values matches accumulate
(lambda xs: hillis_steele(xs, max)[-1] == list(__import__("itertools").accumulate(xs, max)))([(i * 37) % 101 for i in range(100)])
--- check test | Does not change xs
(lambda xs: (hillis_steele(xs, lambda a, b: a + b), xs)[1] == [1, 2, 3])([1, 2, 3])

+++ practice | Parallel radix sort, built from scans
--- task
Radix sort (from DSA I) sorts whole numbers by one bit at a time, from the lowest bit up, with a **stable** split at each bit: every key whose bit is 0 moves before every key whose bit is 1, keeping their order within each group. A parallel machine does the split with scans:

- the flag of key j is 1 if its bit is 0; the exclusive scan of these flags gives each 0-key its position;
- let `zeros` be the number of 0-keys. A 1-key's position is `zeros` plus the exclusive scan of the opposite flags (1 when the bit is 1) at its index.

Write `split_positions(keys, bit)` that returns the list of destination positions, one per key, computed as above (bit number `bit`, where bit 0 is the lowest: `(key >> bit) & 1`). Write your own exclusive scan helper for it. Then write `radix_sort(keys, bits)` that sorts non-negative whole numbers below 2^bits by splitting on bits 0, 1, …, bits − 1, each time moving every key to its position. Do not change `keys`.

For example, `split_positions([5, 2, 7, 4], 0)` is `[2, 0, 3, 1]`: 2 and 4 have bit 0 equal to 0 and go to positions 0 and 1, then 5 and 7 go to 2 and 3.
--- starter
def split_positions(keys, bit):
    return list(range(len(keys)))


def radix_sort(keys, bits):
    return list(keys)
--- solution
def exclusive_sums(flags):
    out = []
    total = 0
    for f in flags:
        out.append(total)
        total += f
    return out


def split_positions(keys, bit):
    ones = [(k >> bit) & 1 for k in keys]
    zero_flags = [1 - b for b in ones]
    zero_pos = exclusive_sums(zero_flags)
    one_pos = exclusive_sums(ones)
    zeros = sum(zero_flags)
    return [zero_pos[j] if ones[j] == 0 else zeros + one_pos[j] for j in range(len(keys))]


def radix_sort(keys, bits):
    current = list(keys)
    for bit in range(bits):
        pos = split_positions(current, bit)
        nxt = [None] * len(current)
        for j, key in enumerate(current):
            nxt[pos[j]] = key
        current = nxt
    return current
--- hint
Make two lists of flags: `ones[j] = (keys[j] >> bit) & 1`, and the zero flags `1 - ones[j]`. Scan both with an exclusive sum.
--- hint
A 0-key's position is its zero-flag scan value; a 1-key's is the number of zeros plus its one-flag scan value.
--- hint
In `radix_sort`, each pass builds a new list of the same length and puts each key at its position (a scatter). Repeat for every bit from 0 up.
--- check case | The example from the task
split_positions([5, 2, 7, 4], 0)
=> [2, 0, 3, 1]
--- check case | Splitting on a higher bit
split_positions([5, 2, 7, 4, 1], 1)
=> [0, 3, 4, 1, 2]
--- check case | All zeros or all ones on this bit
(split_positions([4, 8, 12], 0), split_positions([1, 3, 5], 0))
=> ([0, 1, 2], [0, 1, 2])
--- check case | An empty list
(split_positions([], 3), radix_sort([], 4))
=> ([], [])
--- check case | radix_sort sorts, duplicates included
radix_sort([13, 2, 9, 2, 15, 0, 7, 9], 4)
=> [0, 2, 2, 7, 9, 9, 13, 15]
--- check test | 2,000 keys of 12 bits, input unchanged
(lambda ks: radix_sort(ks, 12) == sorted(ks) and ks[0] == 0 and ks[1] == 2567 % 4096)([(i * 2567) % 4096 for i in range(2000)])

+++ practice | Segmented scan
--- task
A **segmented scan** restarts at the start of each segment: the running sums of several telemetry passes stored end to end. It is still a single scan, over pairs `(flag, value)`, with this operator:

`seg_op((f1, v1), (f2, v2))` is `(f1 or f2, v2)` if `f2` is true, and `(f1 or f2, v1 + v2)` otherwise.

In words: if the right item starts a segment, everything to its left is forgotten.

Write `seg_op(a, b)` for pairs of a bool and a number, and `segmented_sums(values, starts)` that returns the list of inclusive running sums restarting at every position where `starts` is `True`. Position 0 always starts a segment, whatever `starts[0]` says. Compute it as an inclusive scan with `seg_op` (a loop is fine), then keep only the values. An empty list gives `[]`.

For example, `segmented_sums([1, 2, 3, 4, 5], [True, False, True, False, False])` is `[1, 3, 3, 7, 12]`.

A check tests that `seg_op` is associative on every triple of a small set of pairs, which is why this scan can run in parallel.
--- starter
def seg_op(a, b):
    return (a[0] or b[0], a[1] + b[1])


def segmented_sums(values, starts):
    out = []
    total = 0
    for v in values:
        total += v
        out.append(total)
    return out
--- solution
def seg_op(a, b):
    f1, v1 = a
    f2, v2 = b
    if f2:
        return (True, v2)
    return (f1, v1 + v2)


def segmented_sums(values, starts):
    out = []
    acc = None
    for i, v in enumerate(values):
        item = (i == 0 or bool(starts[i]), v)
        acc = item if acc is None else seg_op(acc, item)
        out.append(acc[1])
    return out
--- hint
In `seg_op`, if the right pair's flag is true, return `(True, v2)`; otherwise return `(f1, v1 + v2)`. Either way the flag is "f1 or f2".
--- hint
In `segmented_sums`, turn each value into a pair whose flag is `True` at position 0 or where `starts` says so. Scan the pairs: the running value starts as the first pair, and each next pair is combined with `seg_op(running, pair)`.
--- check case | The example from the task
segmented_sums([1, 2, 3, 4, 5], [True, False, True, False, False])
=> [1, 3, 3, 7, 12]
--- check case | Position 0 starts a segment even when marked False
segmented_sums([4, 1, 6], [False, False, True])
=> [4, 5, 6]
--- check case | Every position starts a segment
segmented_sums([3, -2, 9], [True, True, True])
=> [3, -2, 9]
--- check case | No marked starts: one running sum
segmented_sums([2, 2, 2, 2], [False, False, False, False])
=> [2, 4, 6, 8]
--- check case | Empty list
segmented_sums([], [])
=> []
--- check case | seg_op on its own
(seg_op((False, 5), (False, 3)), seg_op((False, 5), (True, 3)), seg_op((True, 5), (False, 3)))
=> ((False, 8), (True, 3), (True, 8))
--- check test | seg_op is associative on every triple
(lambda P: all(seg_op(seg_op(a, b), c) == seg_op(a, seg_op(b, c)) for a in P for b in P for c in P))([(f, v) for f in (False, True) for v in (-3, 0, 2, 7)])
?? If seg_op were not associative, a parallel scan would give a different answer from the loop.

+++ practice | Debug: the heat that leaks forwards
--- task
`diffuse(temps, steps)` models heat spreading along a rod. In each step, every inner cell i (not the first or the last, which stay fixed) becomes `(old[i - 1] + 2 * old[i] + old[i + 1]) // 4`, using the temperatures from **before** the step. Return the list after `steps` steps, without changing `temps`.

The program gives the wrong answer. For `diffuse([0, 0, 80, 0, 0], 1)` it should give `[0, 20, 40, 20, 0]`, but it gives `[0, 20, 45, 11, 0]`: the heat seems to leak towards the right end. Find the bug and fix it.
--- starter
def diffuse(temps, steps):
    a = list(temps)
    for _ in range(steps):
        for i in range(1, len(a) - 1):
            a[i] = (a[i - 1] + 2 * a[i] + a[i + 1]) // 4
    return a
--- solution
def diffuse(temps, steps):
    a = list(temps)
    for _ in range(steps):
        new = list(a)
        for i in range(1, len(a) - 1):
            new[i] = (a[i - 1] + 2 * a[i] + a[i + 1]) // 4
        a = new
    return a
--- hint
When the loop computes cell 2, which value of cell 1 does it read: the one from before the step, or the one it just wrote?
--- hint
Keep two lists: read every neighbour from the old list and write into a new one, then make the new list the current one after the step. This is double buffering.
--- check case | The example from the task
diffuse([0, 0, 80, 0, 0], 1)
=> [0, 20, 40, 20, 0]
--- check case | Two steps
diffuse([0, 0, 80, 0, 0], 2)
=> [0, 20, 30, 20, 0]
--- check case | Symmetric input stays symmetric
diffuse([100, 0, 0, 0, 0, 0, 100], 3)
=> [100, 45, 12, 3, 12, 45, 100]
--- check case | Rods too short to have inner cells, and zero steps
(diffuse([5, 9], 4), diffuse([], 2), diffuse([1, 8, 1], 0))
=> ([5, 9], [], [1, 8, 1])
--- check test | Does not change the input
(lambda t: (diffuse(t, 3), t)[1] == [0, 40, 0])([0, 40, 0])

+++ practice | A blocked scan on p workers
--- task
A real multicore scans in three phases. Split `xs` into p contiguous blocks as evenly as possible: the first `len(xs) % p` blocks get one extra item, and empty blocks (when p is larger than the list) are dropped. Then:

1. each worker computes the inclusive running sums of its own block with a loop;
2. one worker computes, for each block, the total of all blocks before it (an exclusive scan of the block totals);
3. each worker except the first adds its block's offset to every item of its block.

Write `blocked_scan(xs, p)` that returns the pair `(sums, span)`: the inclusive running sums of the whole list, and the span in additions. The span is the longest block's additions in phase 1 (its length − 1), plus the additions in phase 2 (number of blocks − 1), plus the longest block's additions in phase 3 (the length of the longest block other than the first, or 0 if there is only one block). An empty list gives `([], 0)`.

For example, `blocked_scan([3, 1, 4, 1, 5, 9, 2], 3)` splits into `[3, 1, 4]`, `[1, 5]`, `[9, 2]`, and returns `([3, 4, 8, 9, 14, 23, 25], 6)`: 2 additions in phase 1, 2 in phase 2, and 2 in phase 3.
--- starter
def blocked_scan(xs, p):
    sums = []
    total = 0
    for x in xs:
        total += x
        sums.append(total)
    return (sums, len(xs) - 1)
--- solution
def blocked_scan(xs, p):
    n = len(xs)
    if n == 0:
        return ([], 0)
    size, extra = divmod(n, p)
    blocks = []
    start = 0
    for k in range(p):
        length = size + (1 if k < extra else 0)
        if length > 0:
            blocks.append(xs[start:start + length])
        start += length
    scanned = []
    for block in blocks:
        run = []
        total = 0
        for x in block:
            total += x
            run.append(total)
        scanned.append(run)
    offsets = []
    before = 0
    for run in scanned:
        offsets.append(before)
        before += run[-1]
    sums = []
    for run, off in zip(scanned, offsets):
        sums.extend(v + off for v in run)
    phase1 = max(len(b) for b in blocks) - 1
    phase2 = len(blocks) - 1
    phase3 = max((len(b) for b in blocks[1:]), default=0)
    return (sums, phase1 + phase2 + phase3)
--- hint
`divmod(n, p)` gives the base block size and how many blocks get one extra item. Walk a start index along the list to cut the blocks, and skip blocks of length 0.
--- hint
Phase 1 is a running sum per block. Phase 2 walks the blocks in order, recording the total so far before adding each block's last running sum. Phase 3 adds each block's offset to its running sums.
--- hint
For the span, take the longest block's length − 1, plus the number of blocks − 1, plus the longest length among the blocks after the first (use `default=0` for a single block).
--- check case | The example from the task
blocked_scan([3, 1, 4, 1, 5, 9, 2], 3)
=> ([3, 4, 8, 9, 14, 23, 25], 6)
--- check case | One worker is a plain loop
blocked_scan([2, 2, 2, 2], 1)
=> ([2, 4, 6, 8], 3)
--- check case | More workers than items: empty blocks are dropped
blocked_scan([5, -1, 4], 8)
=> ([5, 4, 8], 3)
--- check case | Empty list
blocked_scan([], 4)
=> ([], 0)
--- check case | Even blocks
blocked_scan(list(range(1, 13)), 4)
=> ([1, 3, 6, 10, 15, 21, 28, 36, 45, 55, 66, 78], 8)
--- check test | 100,000 readings on 16 workers
(lambda xs: (lambda r: r[0] == list(__import__("itertools").accumulate(xs)) and r[1] == 6250 - 1 + 15 + 6250)(blocked_scan(xs, 16)))([(i * 7) % 13 - 6 for i in range(100000)])
=== par-04 | Threads in C++: join, data races, happens-before and std::atomic
--- teach
Last lesson's patterns described parallel work as rounds and trees. Real parallel programs on a CPU run as threads that share memory. In Operating Systems you started threads with `std::thread` and protected shared data with `std::mutex`. This lesson goes one level deeper, to what the C++ language actually promises when threads share memory. The answer is a precise rule, **happens-before**, and a precise definition of the bug it forbids, the **data race**. By the end you will have built a race detector that decides, from a record of what the threads did, exactly which pairs of memory accesses race.

### The life of a std::thread

A `std::thread` object starts running a function the moment it is constructed. The arguments are **copied** into the new thread, even if the function takes a reference; to pass a real reference, wrap the argument in `std::ref`:

```cpp fragment
void fill(std::vector<int>& out, int n);

std::vector<int> buf;
std::thread t(fill, std::ref(buf), 1000);   // without std::ref, fill would get a copy
t.join();                                     // wait until fill has returned
```

The object is **joinable** while it represents a thread that has not been joined or detached. The rules:

- `join()` waits for the thread to finish; afterwards the object is not joinable.
- `detach()` lets the thread run on alone; the object is no longer joinable, and you can never wait for that thread again.
- A default-constructed `std::thread`, and one that has been moved from, is not joinable.
- Calling `join()` or `detach()` on an object that is not joinable is an error (it throws `std::system_error`; in ORBIT's C++, which has no exceptions, the program aborts).
- **If a joinable `std::thread` is destroyed, or assigned a new thread, the program calls `std::terminate` and dies.** The language refuses to guess whether you meant to wait.

`std::thread` objects can be moved but not copied, since two objects cannot both own one thread: `b = std::move(a);` hands a's thread to b and leaves a not joinable (and terminates if b was joinable). C++20's [[std::jthread|jthread]] joins automatically in its destructor, which removes the most common way to hit `std::terminate`.

### Conflicting accesses and data races

Two memory accesses **conflict** if they touch the same **memory location** and at least one of them is a write. A memory location is one scalar object: an `int`, a pointer, one element of an array, one field of a struct. So two threads writing two different elements of one array do not conflict. Two reads never conflict.

A **data race** is a pair of conflicting accesses, made by different threads, where at least one is not atomic, and **neither happens before the other**. The C++ standard's verdict on a program with a data race is short: its behaviour is **undefined**. Not "the counter might be a bit off". Undefined, as in Systems Programming: the compiler may assume data races never happen, and optimise on that basis. For example, it may keep a variable in a register instead of reading it from memory again:

```cpp fragment
bool done = false;                 // plain bool, written by another thread

void wait_for_done() {
    while (!done) { }              // the compiler may read done once and loop forever
}
```

No other thread can change `done` without a data race, so as far as the compiler is concerned, nothing in the loop can change it.

In everyday words: if two threads touch the same variable, one of them writing, something must force an order between them. The next section says what counts.

### Happens-before

Within one thread, each action is **sequenced before** the next one, in program order. Between threads, certain pairs of actions **synchronise with** each other:

1. **Starting a thread**: the construction of a `std::thread` synchronises with the start of the new thread's function.
2. **Joining**: the end of a thread's function synchronises with the return from `join()` on it.
3. **Mutexes**: unlocking a mutex synchronises with the next lock of the same mutex, by any thread.
4. **Atomics**: an atomic store with release semantics synchronises with an atomic load with acquire semantics that reads the value it stored. (The default for `std::atomic` operations has both.)

**Happens-before** is the smallest relation that contains "sequenced before" and "synchronises with" and is **transitive**: if A happens before B and B happens before C, then A happens before C.

That last word makes happens-before a question about graphs. Draw each action as a node, and each program-order step and each synchronisation as an edge. Then **A happens before B exactly when there is a path from A to B**. Here is a record of a run, one action per line, in the order they happened:

| # | thread | action |
|---|---|---|
| 0 | main | write `cfg` |
| 1 | main | start thread W |
| 2 | W | read `cfg` |
| 3 | W | write `result` |
| 4 | main | read `result` |
| 5 | main | join W |
| 6 | main | read `result` |

- Accesses 0 and 2 conflict, and 0 → 1 (program order) → 2 (thread start) is a path. No race.
- Accesses 3 and 4 conflict, and there is no path either way: nothing forced main to wait for W. **Data race.**
- Accesses 3 and 6 conflict, but 3 → 5 (W's last action, then the join) → 6 is a path. No race.

The same run in C++:

```cpp fragment
int cfg = 0, result = 0;

int main() {
    cfg = 7;                                        // 0
    std::thread w([] { result = cfg * 2; });        // 1, then 2 and 3 inside W
    int early = result;                             // 4: races with W's write
    w.join();                                       // 5
    int late = result;                              // 6: fine, and always 14
}
```

Notice that a race is about **the program**, not about luck: access 4 races with access 3 whether or not, in this run, W happened to finish first. The record is one possible order; the race is there because a different order was equally allowed.

### Why the hardware needs a rule

A single CPU core runs your instructions in order, as far as that core can tell. Other cores can see them in a different order. The main reason is the **[[store buffer|store-buffer]]**: a core that writes to memory puts the write in a small private queue and carries on, and the write reaches the cache a little later. The core's own later reads check its queue, so it sees its own writes at once; other cores do not see them yet.

The classic demonstration is the **store-buffering** test. Two variables start at 0:

| thread 1 | thread 2 |
|---|---|
| x = 1 | y = 1 |
| r1 = y | r2 = x |

Can both threads read 0? Under **[[sequential consistency|sc]]**, where the result is as if all actions ran one at a time in a single interleaving that keeps each thread's order, no:

1. Suppose r1 = 0. Then thread 1's read of y came before thread 2's write of y, or it would have seen 1.
2. Thread 1's write of x came before its own read of y (program order). With step 1, the write of x came before the write of y.
3. Thread 2's read of x comes after its own write of y (program order), so after the write of x. So r2 = 1.
4. The same argument with the threads swapped shows that r2 = 0 forces r1 = 1. So (0, 0) never happens.

But on real x86 and ARM processors, both reads **can** return 0: both writes sit in store buffers while both reads go to memory. With plain `int`s this program has data races, so C++ promises nothing at all. With atomics, it depends on the memory order you ask for, as the next sections show.

### std::atomic

An object of type `std::atomic<T>` can be read and written by many threads at once without a data race. Each operation on it is **indivisible**: no thread ever sees it half done.

- `load()` and `store(v)` read and write.
- `fetch_add(v)` adds v and returns the **old** value, as one indivisible step: a counter updated with `fetch_add` never loses an update. So does `++` on an atomic.
- `exchange(v)` stores v and returns the old value.
- `compare_exchange_strong(expected, desired)`, **compare-and-swap** (CAS): if the value equals `expected`, replace it with `desired` and return `true`; otherwise copy the current value into `expected` and return `false`. All in one indivisible step.

This program runs on one thread, just to show what each operation returns:

```cpp
#include <atomic>
#include <cstdio>

int main() {
    std::atomic<int> hits{10};
    int before = hits.fetch_add(5);
    std::printf("%d %d\n", before, hits.load());

    int expected = 12;
    bool ok = hits.compare_exchange_strong(expected, 99);
    std::printf("%d %d %d\n", ok, expected, hits.load());

    ok = hits.compare_exchange_strong(expected, 99);
    std::printf("%d %d %d\n", ok, expected, hits.load());
}
```

It prints `10 15`, then `0 15 15` (the CAS failed, and `expected` was updated to the real value, 15), then `1 15 99`.

CAS is the universal tool: any update "new value = f(old value)" can be made atomic with a **CAS loop**. Read the value, compute f of it, and try to swap it in; if another thread changed it in between, the CAS fails, and you try again with the fresh value:

```cpp fragment
void record_peak(std::atomic<int>& peak, int reading) {
    int seen = peak.load();
    while (seen < reading && !peak.compare_exchange_weak(seen, reading)) {
        // seen now holds the current value; the loop test runs again
    }
}
```

The loop cannot fail forever: a CAS fails only because another thread's update succeeded, so the threads as a whole always make progress. `compare_exchange_weak` may fail even when the values match, which is why it lives in loops; on some processors it is faster.

### Memory orders

Each atomic operation takes an optional **memory order**, which says how much ordering it creates.

- **`memory_order_seq_cst`** (the default): all such operations appear in one total order that every thread agrees on, consistent with each thread's program order. The key theorem, called **DRF-SC**, says: a program with no data races whose atomics are all seq_cst behaves as if it were sequentially consistent. In the store-buffering test with seq_cst atomics, r1 = r2 = 0 is impossible, by exactly the argument above.
- **`memory_order_relaxed`**: the operation is indivisible, and nothing more. It creates no happens-before edges. A counter of processed frames that is only read after every thread is joined can use `fetch_add(1, std::memory_order_relaxed)`: the joins provide the ordering.
- **`memory_order_release`** on a store and **`memory_order_acquire`** on a load: when the load reads the value the store wrote, the store synchronises with the load. Everything the storing thread did before the store happens before everything the loading thread does after the load.

Release and acquire are weaker than seq_cst. In the store-buffering test they still allow r1 = r2 = 0: each load reads the initial 0, not the other thread's store, so no store synchronises with any load and nothing is ordered. But release and acquire are exactly what **message passing** needs:

```cpp fragment
Frame frame;                          // plain data
std::atomic<bool> ready{false};

void producer() {
    frame = decode_next();            // 1: plain write
    ready.store(true, std::memory_order_release);          // 2
}

void consumer() {
    while (!ready.load(std::memory_order_acquire)) { }     // 3: reads true
    process(frame);                   // 4: plain read, no race
}
```

1 → 2 is program order, 2 → 3 is release-acquire synchronisation (3 read the value 2 wrote), and 3 → 4 is program order. So 1 happens before 4, and the consumer sees the whole frame. With relaxed operations there would be no 2 → 3 edge, 1 and 4 would race, and the consumer could see a half-written frame.

An atomic **read-modify-write** such as `fetch_add` with the default order both acquires and releases: it synchronises with the store or read-modify-write it read from, and the next one reads from it. So a chain of `fetch_add`s passes happens-before along from thread to thread.

**Cost.** An uncontended atomic operation costs a few nanoseconds; a seq_cst store on x86 needs a full fence that drains the store buffer, which costs tens of cycles; relaxed and acquire-release operations are cheaper on some processors. When many cores update the same atomic, each update must take the variable's cache line away from the other cores, and the cost rises to tens or hundreds of nanoseconds. Lesson 5 is about that.

### Finding races from a record

A race detector records what the threads do, builds the happens-before graph, and reports every pair of conflicting accesses from different threads with no path between them. It finds races that did not cause a wrong answer in this run but could in another. Tools like [[ThreadSanitizer|tsan]] do exactly this, with a faster representation of the graph called vector clocks, which lesson 9 builds.

To build the graph from a record in the order things happened, walk it once and remember a few "latest" things:

- the latest action of each thread (for program-order edges);
- for each thread that has been started but has not acted yet, the start action (for the start edge);
- for each mutex, its latest unlock (for the edge to the next lock);
- for each atomic variable, its latest store or read-modify-write (a load, or a read-modify-write, reads the value of the latest one, so it gets an edge from it).

Every edge goes from an earlier action in the record to a later one, so one pass is enough.

### Where it is used

- **Every multithreaded C++, C, Java, Rust and Go program** relies on the same idea: the languages adopted happens-before models around 2004 to 2011.
- **Lock-free data structures**, such as the queues that pass telemetry between threads in flight software, are built from atomics and CAS loops.
- **ThreadSanitizer** runs in the test suites of Chrome, Firefox and many servers, finding races before they ship.

**Watch out:**

- **`volatile` is not atomic.** In C++ it only stops the compiler from removing or merging accesses; it gives no indivisibility and no happens-before. Use `std::atomic`.
- **Check-then-act on an atomic is still a race of logic.** `if (slots.load() > 0) slots.fetch_sub(1);` can let two threads both see 1 and both subtract. Make the whole decision one CAS.
- **Forgetting to join.** A joinable `std::thread` that goes out of scope calls `std::terminate`. A detached thread that uses a local variable of a function that has returned reads freed memory.
- **Relaxed where ordering is needed.** A relaxed flag does not publish the data written before it.
- **"It passed the tests".** A data race is undefined behaviour even in the runs where the answer came out right.

::: context jthread A thread that joins itself
`std::jthread`, added in C++20, is a `std::thread` whose destructor asks the thread to stop and then joins it, so forgetting to join no longer kills the program. It also carries a stop token: the thread's function can take a `std::stop_token` and check `stop_requested()` in its loop, which gives a standard way to ask a worker to finish. Prefer it for new code when your compiler supports it; `std::thread` remains everywhere in existing code.
:::

::: context store-buffer A private queue of writes
Each core keeps its recent writes in a store buffer before they reach the shared cache. Its own reads look in the buffer first, so it sees its own writes; other cores only see them once they drain. That is how both reads in the store-buffering test can return 0.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 320 170" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="20" y="10" width="110" height="34" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1"/>
  <text x="75" y="31" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">core 1: r1 = y</text>
  <rect x="190" y="10" width="110" height="34" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1"/>
  <text x="245" y="31" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">core 2: r2 = x</text>
  <rect x="20" y="62" width="110" height="26" fill="#f6d28b" stroke="#b07a12"/>
  <text x="75" y="80" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">buffer: x = 1</text>
  <rect x="190" y="62" width="110" height="26" fill="#f6d28b" stroke="#b07a12"/>
  <text x="245" y="80" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">buffer: y = 1</text>
  <rect x="60" y="120" width="200" height="34" fill="#dfe6f1" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="160" y="141" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">memory: x = 0, y = 0</text>
  <line x1="75" y1="44" x2="75" y2="62" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <line x1="245" y1="44" x2="245" y2="62" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <line x1="75" y1="88" x2="120" y2="120" stroke="#b07a12" stroke-dasharray="3 3"/>
  <line x1="245" y1="88" x2="200" y2="120" stroke="#b07a12" stroke-dasharray="3 3"/>
  <text x="160" y="110" font-size="10" text-anchor="middle" fill="#b4232c">writes not drained yet: both reads get 0</text>

```
:::

::: context sc One interleaving for everyone
Leslie Lamport defined sequential consistency in 1979: the result of any run is the same as if the operations of all the processors ran in some single sequential order, with each processor's operations in the order its program gives. It is the model everyone imagines when they first think about threads, and the one your interleaving explorer in Operating Systems assumed. Hardware does not provide it for plain accesses, because store buffers and caches would be much slower if it did; languages provide it for race-free programs instead.
:::

::: context tsan A race detector in your build
ThreadSanitizer is built into clang and GCC: compile with `-fsanitize=thread`, run your tests, and it reports each data race with the two stack traces involved. It keeps a vector clock per thread and a few recent accesses per 8 bytes of memory, which slows the program by about 5 to 15 times. It only sees races on code paths the tests actually run, but on those it needs no lucky timing: the race is reported even if the bad order never happened.
:::
--- task
Write `find_races(trace)`. A **trace** is a list of events in the order they happened in one run. Each event is a tuple `(thread, action, target)`, where `thread` and `target` are strings. The actions are:

- `"read"` and `"write"`: plain (non-atomic) accesses to the variable `target`;
- `"spawn"`: this thread starts the thread named `target`;
- `"join"`: this thread waits for the thread named `target` to finish (all of its events come earlier in the trace);
- `"lock"` and `"unlock"`: of the mutex `target`;
- `"store"`, `"load"` and `"rmw"`: atomic operations on the atomic variable `target`, with the default (seq_cst) order.

Build the happens-before graph with these edges, and nothing else:

1. each event to the next event of the same thread;
2. a `spawn` to the first event of the spawned thread;
3. the last event of a thread to every `join` of that thread;
4. an `unlock` of a mutex to the next `lock` of the same mutex;
5. the latest `store` or `rmw` of an atomic variable to each later `load` or `rmw` of it that comes before the next `store` or `rmw` (it reads that value).

Return the sorted list of pairs `(i, j)` of event positions, `i < j`, such that events i and j are both plain accesses to the same variable, from different threads, at least one a write, and there is **no path** from i to j in the graph. Atomic operations never race. A check uses a trace of about 1,200 events, so find everything reachable from each access once, not once per pair.

For example, in the trace `[("A", "write", "v"), ("B", "read", "v")]` nothing orders the two accesses, so the answer is `[(0, 1)]`.
--- starter
def find_races(trace):
    return []
--- solution
def build_edges(trace):
    succ = [[] for _ in trace]
    last = {}
    waiting = {}
    last_unlock = {}
    last_write = {}
    for i, (t, act, obj) in enumerate(trace):
        if t in last:
            succ[last[t]].append(i)
        elif t in waiting:
            succ[waiting.pop(t)].append(i)
        last[t] = i
        if act == "spawn":
            waiting[obj] = i
        elif act == "join" and obj in last:
            succ[last[obj]].append(i)
        elif act == "lock" and obj in last_unlock:
            succ[last_unlock[obj]].append(i)
        elif act in ("load", "rmw") and obj in last_write:
            succ[last_write[obj]].append(i)
        if act == "unlock":
            last_unlock[obj] = i
        if act in ("store", "rmw"):
            last_write[obj] = i
    return succ


def reachable(succ, start):
    seen = set()
    stack = [start]
    while stack:
        u = stack.pop()
        for v in succ[u]:
            if v not in seen:
                seen.add(v)
                stack.append(v)
    return seen


def find_races(trace):
    succ = build_edges(trace)
    by_var = {}
    for i, (t, act, obj) in enumerate(trace):
        if act in ("read", "write"):
            by_var.setdefault(obj, []).append(i)
    races = []
    for accesses in by_var.values():
        for a, i in enumerate(accesses):
            later = reachable(succ, i)
            for j in accesses[a + 1:]:
                ti, ai, _ = trace[i]
                tj, aj, _ = trace[j]
                if ti != tj and "write" in (ai, aj) and j not in later:
                    races.append((i, j))
    return sorted(races)
--- hint
Build a list of successors for each event in one pass over the trace. Keep dictionaries for the latest event of each thread, the pending `spawn` of each thread that has not acted yet, the latest `unlock` of each mutex, and the latest `store` or `rmw` of each atomic variable.
--- hint
For each event, first add the program-order edge (or the spawn edge, for a thread's first event). Then add the edge its action needs: from the joined thread's latest event for a `join`, from the latest unlock for a `lock`, from the latest store or rmw for a `load` or `rmw`. Only then update the "latest" records.
--- hint
Group the plain accesses by variable. For each access i, find every event reachable from i with a depth-first search, once. Then every later access j to the same variable, from another thread, with a write among the two, races with i if j is not reachable.
--- check case | The example from the task
find_races([("A", "write", "v"), ("B", "read", "v")])
=> [(0, 1)]
--- check case | Two reads never race
find_races([("A", "read", "v"), ("B", "read", "v")])
=> []
--- check case | One thread never races with itself
find_races([("A", "write", "v"), ("A", "read", "v"), ("A", "write", "v")])
=> []
--- check case | Spawn orders the parent's earlier writes; join orders the child's writes
find_races([("main", "write", "x"), ("main", "spawn", "W"), ("W", "read", "x"), ("W", "write", "y"), ("main", "join", "W"), ("main", "read", "y")])
=> []
--- check case | Reading before the join is a race
find_races([("main", "spawn", "W"), ("W", "write", "y"), ("main", "read", "y"), ("main", "join", "W"), ("main", "read", "y")])
=> [(1, 2)]

find_races([("main", "spawn", "W"), ("main", "write", "x"), ("W", "read", "x")])
=> [(1, 2)]

find_races([("A", "lock", "m"), ("A", "write", "c"), ("A", "unlock", "m"), ("B", "lock", "m"), ("B", "read", "c"), ("B", "write", "c"), ("B", "unlock", "m")])
=> []

--- check case | One thread forgets the lock
find_races([("A", "lock", "m"), ("A", "write", "c"), ("A", "unlock", "m"), ("B", "write", "c"), ("C", "lock", "m"), ("C", "read", "c"), ("C", "unlock", "m")])

find_races([("A", "write", "p"), ("B", "write", "q"), ("B", "read", "p"), ("A", "read", "q"), ("C", "write", "p")])
=> [(0, 2), (0, 4), (1, 3), (2, 4)]
--- check case | An empty trace
find_races([])
=> []
--- check case | 1,197 events: one unlocked peek among 299 locked updates
find_races([e for k in range(300) for e in ([("W%d" % (k % 4), "lock", "m"), ("W%d" % (k % 4), "read", "c"), ("W%d" % (k % 4), "write", "c"), ("W%d" % (k % 4), "unlock", "m")] if k != 150 else [("W2", "read", "c")])])
=> [(590, 600), (594, 600), (598, 600), (600, 603), (600, 607), (600, 611)]
?? Find the events reachable from each access once, then test every later access against that set.

+++ question | Which pair is a data race?
--- ask
Thread A runs `x = 1; flag.store(true, std::memory_order_relaxed);` and thread B runs `while (!flag.load(std::memory_order_relaxed)) {} int r = x;`, where `x` is a plain `int` and `flag` a `std::atomic<bool>`. Is there a data race?
--- choice
No: B only reads `x` after it has seen the flag, so the write is finished.
--- choice correct
Yes: relaxed operations create no synchronisation, so nothing makes `x = 1` happen before `int r = x`.
--- choice
No: accesses to atomics never race.
--- choice
Yes, but only on processors without store buffers.
--- why
The flag itself is atomic and never races, but `x` is plain. For the write of `x` to happen before the read, the store must synchronise with the load, and that needs release on the store and acquire on the load (or the seq_cst default). Relaxed gives indivisibility only, so the program has undefined behaviour.

+++ question | What join promises
--- ask
Worker threads each write their own slot of a plain `std::vector<int> totals`, and `main` joins all of them and then sums `totals`. Why is `main`'s reading safe?
--- choice
Because each worker writes a different element, so there are no conflicts at all.
--- choice correct
Because the end of each worker synchronises with the return of its `join()`, so every worker's writes happen before `main`'s reads.
--- choice
Because `std::vector` is thread-safe.
--- choice
Because the workers have finished, so their store buffers are empty.
--- why
The workers do not conflict with each other (different elements), but each of them conflicts with `main`, which reads every element. The join edge is what orders them. Store buffers are a hardware detail; the language guarantee is the happens-before edge, and it is what stops the compiler from moving the reads earlier, too.

+++ question | volatile and atomic
--- ask
Why does `volatile int count;` not make `count++` safe from two threads?
--- choice
It does make it safe; `volatile` tells the compiler to use atomic instructions.
--- choice correct
`volatile` only stops the compiler from removing or merging the accesses; the increment is still a separate load and store, with no happens-before, so updates can be lost and it is a data race.
--- choice
Because `volatile` variables are kept in registers.
--- why
In C++, `volatile` exists for memory-mapped hardware registers, where every read and write must really happen. It says nothing about indivisibility or ordering between threads. `std::atomic<int>` with `fetch_add` gives both.

+++ practice | Every result under sequential consistency
--- task
A **litmus test** is a tiny two-thread program used to probe a memory model. Each thread is a list of instructions, either `("store", var, value)` or `("load", var, reg)`, which reads `var` into the register named `reg`. Every variable starts at 0, and each register name is loaded exactly once in the whole test.

Under sequential consistency, a run is some interleaving of the two lists that keeps each thread's own order. Write `sc_outcomes(t1, t2)` that tries every interleaving and returns the sorted list of distinct outcomes. An outcome is a tuple of register values, ordered by register name (sorted as strings).

For example, for the store-buffering test `t1 = [("store", "x", 1), ("load", "y", "r1")]` and `t2 = [("store", "y", 1), ("load", "x", "r2")]`, the answer is `[(0, 1), (1, 0), (1, 1)]`: never `(0, 0)`.
--- starter
def sc_outcomes(t1, t2):
    return []
--- solution
def sc_outcomes(t1, t2):
    found = set()

    def run(i, j, mem, regs):
        if i == len(t1) and j == len(t2):
            found.add(tuple(regs[r] for r in sorted(regs)))
            return
        for which, k in ((0, i), (1, j)):
            prog = t1 if which == 0 else t2
            if k == len(prog):
                continue
            op, var, arg = prog[k]
            mem2 = dict(mem)
            regs2 = dict(regs)
            if op == "store":
                mem2[var] = arg
            else:
                regs2[arg] = mem.get(var, 0)
            if which == 0:
                run(i + 1, j, mem2, regs2)
            else:
                run(i, j + 1, mem2, regs2)

    run(0, 0, {}, {})
    return sorted(found)
--- hint
Recurse on the pair (how many of t1's instructions have run, how many of t2's). At each point, either thread with instructions left may take the next one.
--- hint
Pass copies of the memory and register dictionaries down each branch, so one branch's changes never leak into another. A load reads `mem.get(var, 0)`. When both threads are done, record the tuple of register values in sorted register-name order.
--- check case | Store buffering never gives (0, 0)
sc_outcomes([("store", "x", 1), ("load", "y", "r1")], [("store", "y", 1), ("load", "x", "r2")])
=> [(0, 1), (1, 0), (1, 1)]
--- check case | Message passing: seeing the flag means seeing the data
sc_outcomes([("store", "data", 42), ("store", "flag", 1)], [("load", "flag", "a"), ("load", "data", "b")])
=> [(0, 0), (0, 42), (1, 42)]
--- check case | Load buffering never gives (1, 1)
sc_outcomes([("load", "x", "r1"), ("store", "y", 1)], [("load", "y", "r2"), ("store", "x", 1)])
=> [(0, 0), (0, 1), (1, 0)]
--- check case | Two writes to one variable, read twice
sc_outcomes([("store", "x", 1), ("store", "x", 2)], [("load", "x", "p"), ("load", "x", "q")])
=> [(0, 0), (0, 1), (0, 2), (1, 1), (1, 2), (2, 2)]
?? The reads can never see 2 and then 1: the writes happen in order.
--- check case | An empty thread
sc_outcomes([("store", "x", 5), ("load", "x", "r")], [])
=> [(5,)]
--- check case | Register names are sorted as strings
sc_outcomes([("load", "x", "zz")], [("store", "x", 3), ("load", "x", "aa")])
=> [(3, 0), (3, 3)]

+++ practice | What happens to this std::thread?
--- task
Model the rules for `std::thread` objects. A program is a list of operations on named thread objects, as strings:

- `"start a"`: `a = std::thread(f);` If `a` exists and is joinable, the program calls `std::terminate`. Otherwise `a` now exists and is joinable.
- `"declare a"`: `std::thread a;` (a new, non-joinable object).
- `"join a"` and `"detach a"`: if `a` is not joinable (or does not exist), this is an error; otherwise `a` becomes non-joinable.
- `"move a b"`: `b = std::move(a);` If `b` exists and is joinable, `std::terminate`. Otherwise `b` takes over whatever `a` held (joinable or not), and `a` exists and is non-joinable.
- `"end"`: every object is destroyed. If any is joinable, `std::terminate`.

Write `thread_program(ops)` that returns `"terminate at k"` or `"error at k"` for the first operation that goes wrong (k counts operations from 1), or `"ok"` if nothing does. The program stops at the first problem.

For example, `thread_program(["start a", "end"])` is `"terminate at 2"`: `a` is still joinable when it is destroyed.
--- starter
def thread_program(ops):
    return "ok"
--- solution
def thread_program(ops):
    joinable = {}
    for k, op in enumerate(ops, start=1):
        words = op.split()
        cmd = words[0]
        if cmd == "start":
            if joinable.get(words[1], False):
                return f"terminate at {k}"
            joinable[words[1]] = True
        elif cmd == "declare":
            joinable[words[1]] = False
        elif cmd in ("join", "detach"):
            if not joinable.get(words[1], False):
                return f"error at {k}"
            joinable[words[1]] = False
        elif cmd == "move":
            a, b = words[1], words[2]
            if joinable.get(b, False):
                return f"terminate at {k}"
            joinable[b] = joinable.get(a, False)
            joinable[a] = False
        elif cmd == "end":
            if any(joinable.values()):
                return f"terminate at {k}"
    return "ok"
--- hint
Keep a dictionary from object name to "is it joinable?". A name that is not in it behaves like a non-joinable object, so `dict.get(name, False)` answers the question for every case.
--- hint
Check the failure condition of each operation **before** changing anything. For `"move a b"`, read a's state before you set a to non-joinable, in case a and b are different names.
--- check case | The example from the task
thread_program(["start a", "end"])
=> "terminate at 2"
--- check case | Join before the end is fine
thread_program(["start a", "start b", "join a", "detach b", "end"])
=> "ok"
--- check case | Joining twice is an error
thread_program(["start a", "join a", "join a", "end"])
=> "error at 3"
--- check case | Detached threads cannot be joined
thread_program(["start a", "detach a", "join a"])
=> "error at 3"
--- check case | Assigning a new thread to a joinable object terminates
thread_program(["start a", "start a"])
=> "terminate at 2"
--- check case | Restarting after a join is fine
thread_program(["start a", "join a", "start a", "join a", "end"])
=> "ok"
--- check case | A moved-from object is not joinable
thread_program(["start a", "move a b", "join a"])
=> "error at 3"
--- check case | Moving into a joinable object terminates
thread_program(["start a", "start b", "move a b"])
=> "terminate at 3"
--- check case | Moving hands over the duty to join
(thread_program(["start a", "move a b", "join b", "end"]), thread_program(["start a", "move a b", "end"]))
=> ("ok", "terminate at 3")
--- check case | Default-constructed objects, and an empty program
(thread_program(["declare t", "end"]), thread_program(["declare t", "join t"]), thread_program([]))
=> ("ok", "error at 2", "ok")

+++ practice | CAS loops in the step simulator
--- task
The starter holds the step-by-step thread explorer from Operating Systems. A thread is a generator: the code between two `yield`s is one atomic step, and `outcomes(setup, key)` tries every interleaving and returns the sorted distinct final values of `mem[key]`. It also holds `cas(mem, key, expected, new)`: if `mem[key] == expected` it sets `mem[key] = new` and returns `True`, otherwise it returns `False`, all inside one step.

Write two thread functions that use **only** loads, CAS and yields, like code built on `compare_exchange`:

- `cas_increment(mem, times)`: add 1 to `mem["count"]`, `times` times. Each attempt is two steps: load the value into a local variable, `yield`; then try `cas(mem, "count", old, old + 1)`, `yield`. If the CAS failed, try again from the load.
- `atomic_max(mem, value)`: make `mem["peak"]` at least `value`. Each attempt: load the value into a local, `yield`. If it is already at least `value`, stop. Otherwise try `cas(mem, "peak", old, value)`, `yield`, and stop if it succeeded, or try again from the load if it failed.

With correct CAS loops, no interleaving loses an update: two threads doing two increments each always end with 4, and a group of `atomic_max` threads always ends with the largest value.
--- starter
def cas(mem, key, expected, new):
    if mem[key] == expected:
        mem[key] = new
        return True
    return False


def step(threads, done, i):
    try:
        next(threads[i])
    except StopIteration:
        done.add(i)


def outcomes(setup, key):
    found = set()

    def explore(prefix):
        mem, threads = setup()
        done = set()
        for i in prefix:
            step(threads, done, i)
        live = [i for i in range(len(threads)) if i not in done]
        if not live:
            found.add(mem[key])
            return
        for i in live:
            explore(prefix + [i])

    explore([])
    return sorted(found)


def cas_increment(mem, times):
    for _ in range(times):
        old = mem["count"]
        yield
        mem["count"] = old + 1
        yield


def atomic_max(mem, value):
    old = mem["peak"]
    yield
    if old < value:
        mem["peak"] = value
    yield
--- solution
def cas(mem, key, expected, new):
    if mem[key] == expected:
        mem[key] = new
        return True
    return False


def step(threads, done, i):
    try:
        next(threads[i])
    except StopIteration:
        done.add(i)


def outcomes(setup, key):
    found = set()

    def explore(prefix):
        mem, threads = setup()
        done = set()
        for i in prefix:
            step(threads, done, i)
        live = [i for i in range(len(threads)) if i not in done]
        if not live:
            found.add(mem[key])
            return
        for i in live:
            explore(prefix + [i])

    explore([])
    return sorted(found)


def cas_increment(mem, times):
    for _ in range(times):
        while True:
            old = mem["count"]
            yield
            ok = cas(mem, "count", old, old + 1)
            yield
            if ok:
                break


def atomic_max(mem, value):
    while True:
        old = mem["peak"]
        yield
        if old >= value:
            return
        ok = cas(mem, "peak", old, value)
        yield
        if ok:
            return
--- hint
The starter's versions load, yield, and then write blindly, so another thread's update in between is lost. Replace the blind write with `cas(...)`, which only writes if the value is still the one you loaded.
--- hint
Wrap each attempt in `while True:`. Load into `old`, `yield`, call `ok = cas(mem, key, old, new)`, `yield`, then `break` (or `return`) if `ok`. A failed CAS goes round the loop and loads the fresh value.
--- check case | Two threads, two increments each: always 4
outcomes(lambda: (lambda mem: (mem, [cas_increment(mem, 2), cas_increment(mem, 2)]))({"count": 0}), "count")
=> [4]
--- check case | Three threads, one increment each: always 3
outcomes(lambda: (lambda mem: (mem, [cas_increment(mem, 1), cas_increment(mem, 1), cas_increment(mem, 1)]))({"count": 10}), "count")
=> [13]
--- check case | Zero increments change nothing
outcomes(lambda: (lambda mem: (mem, [cas_increment(mem, 0), cas_increment(mem, 1)]))({"count": 0}), "count")
=> [1]
--- check case | Three atomic_max threads: always the largest
outcomes(lambda: (lambda mem: (mem, [atomic_max(mem, 5), atomic_max(mem, 9), atomic_max(mem, 7)]))({"peak": 0}), "peak")
=> [9]
--- check case | A peak already above every value stays
outcomes(lambda: (lambda mem: (mem, [atomic_max(mem, 3), atomic_max(mem, 4)]))({"peak": 8}), "peak")
=> [8]
--- check test | A failed CAS is retried: this schedule makes thread 0's first CAS fail
(lambda mem: (lambda th: ([step(th, set(), i) for i in [0, 1, 1, 1, 0, 0, 0, 0, 0]], mem["count"])[1] == 2)([cas_increment(mem, 1), cas_increment(mem, 1)]))({"count": 0})
?? After a failed CAS, go back and load the value again.

+++ practice | Splitting the work between threads
--- task
Before starting k threads on n items, a program gives each thread a contiguous range. Write `chunk_bounds(n, k)` that returns a list of k pairs `(lo, hi)`, each the half-open range lo, lo + 1, …, hi − 1, such that:

- the ranges are in order and cover 0 to n − 1 exactly, with no gaps and no overlaps (so the first lo is 0, each hi is the next lo, and the last hi is n);
- their sizes differ by at most 1, with the bigger ranges first.

When n < k, the last ranges are empty (lo == hi). n is 0 or more and k is 1 or more; n can be huge, so never build a list of n items.

Then write `chunk_sums(data, k)` that returns the list of the k sums each thread would compute over its range of `data` (0 for an empty range).

For example, `chunk_bounds(10, 3)` is `[(0, 4), (4, 7), (7, 10)]`, and `chunk_sums([1, 2, 3, 4, 5], 2)` is `[6, 9]`.
--- starter
def chunk_bounds(n, k):
    size = n // k
    return [(i * size, (i + 1) * size) for i in range(k)]


def chunk_sums(data, k):
    return [sum(data[lo:hi]) for lo, hi in chunk_bounds(len(data), k)]
--- solution
def chunk_bounds(n, k):
    size, extra = divmod(n, k)
    bounds = []
    lo = 0
    for i in range(k):
        hi = lo + size + (1 if i < extra else 0)
        bounds.append((lo, hi))
        lo = hi
    return bounds


def chunk_sums(data, k):
    return [sum(data[lo:hi]) for lo, hi in chunk_bounds(len(data), k)]
--- hint
The starter gives every thread n // k items and forgets the remainder, so the last n % k items belong to nobody.
--- hint
Use `size, extra = divmod(n, k)`: the first `extra` ranges get `size + 1` items and the rest get `size`. Walk `lo` forward, setting each `hi` to `lo` plus that range's size.
--- check case | The examples from the task
(chunk_bounds(10, 3), chunk_sums([1, 2, 3, 4, 5], 2))
=> ([(0, 4), (4, 7), (7, 10)], [6, 9])
--- check case | Divides evenly
chunk_bounds(12, 4)
=> [(0, 3), (3, 6), (6, 9), (9, 12)]
--- check case | Fewer items than threads: empty ranges at the end
chunk_bounds(2, 5)
=> [(0, 1), (1, 2), (2, 2), (2, 2), (2, 2)]
--- check case | No items at all
(chunk_bounds(0, 3), chunk_sums([], 3))
=> ([(0, 0), (0, 0), (0, 0)], [0, 0, 0])
--- check case | One thread takes everything
chunk_bounds(7, 1)
=> [(0, 7)]
--- check case | Huge n
chunk_bounds(10**12 + 5, 4)
