Once such an entry is committed, every entry before it is committed too, by Log Matching.

Why not commit an older-term entry once a majority has it? The Raft paper's [[Figure 8|figure8]] shows the danger, with five servers S1 to S5:

1. S1 leads term 2 and sends the entry at index 2 (term 2) to S2 only. Then it crashes.
2. S5 wins term 3, with votes from S3, S4 and itself, and appends a different index-2 entry, of term 3, only to its own log. It crashes.
3. S1 recovers, wins term 4, and copies its term-2 entry to S3. Now S1, S2 and S3 hold it: a majority.
4. If S1 committed it now and then crashed, S5 could win term 5: its last entry's term, 3, beats the others' 2, so S2, S3 and S4 would vote for it. S5 would then overwrite index 2 everywhere with its term-3 entry, and a "committed" entry would vanish.

With the rule, S1 first appends an entry of term 4 and commits once **that** is on a majority. Then S5 can no longer win, because a majority has a last entry of term 4, which is more up-to-date than S5's.

**Leader completeness: a committed entry is in the log of every later leader.** An entry committed in term T sits on a majority. A later candidate needs votes from a majority, so some voter has the entry. That voter grants its vote only to a candidate at least as up-to-date as itself, and one can show from Log Matching that such a candidate's log contains the entry too. Together with applying only committed entries, in index order, this gives **state machine safety**: no two servers ever apply different commands at the same index.

### What it costs

- **One round trip to a majority per command.** The leader waits for the fastest f + 1 servers, counting itself. Batching many commands into one AppendEntries amortises that.
- **Everything goes through the leader,** which limits throughput. Systems that need more run many Raft groups, one per range of keys: [[multi-Raft|multiraft]].
- **Linearizable reads** cannot just read the leader's copy, which might be a deposed leader's; the leader first confirms with a majority that it is still leader, or holds a time-based lease.
- **Unavailability during elections:** after a leader fails, nothing commits until the timeout fires and a new leader wins, typically a fraction of a second.

### Where it is used

- **etcd**, which stores the whole state of every Kubernetes cluster; **Consul**; **CockroachDB** and **TiKV**, with one Raft group per range of data; **Kafka**'s KRaft controller; **MongoDB**'s replication protocol is closely modelled on it.
- **Paxos**, Leslie Lamport's older algorithm, runs Google's Chubby lock service and Spanner; **ZooKeeper** uses a close relative called Zab.
- **Spacecraft and aircraft** use related ideas for redundant flight computers, often with Byzantine fault tolerance ([[Byzantine consensus|bft]]), since a failing computer there may send wrong values, not just stop.

**Watch out:**

- **Commit only current-term entries by counting.** Older entries become committed indirectly, when a current-term entry after them is.
- **Never truncate on a matching entry.** A delayed AppendEntries must not delete entries a newer one added.
- **Indexes start at 1,** and prevLogIndex 0 always passes the check. Off-by-one errors here corrupt logs.
- **Persist the term, the vote and the log before replying.** A server that forgets its vote after a restart can vote twice in one term.
- **Election timeouts must be random,** and longer than a round trip, or elections keep splitting.
- **An even cluster size adds cost without adding fault tolerance.** Use 3, 5 or 7 servers.

::: context paxos The algorithm Raft replaced
Leslie Lamport described Paxos in a paper written in 1989 and published in 1998, framed as the parliament of a Greek island. It decides a single value in two phases: a proposer first asks a majority to promise to ignore older proposals and to report any value they already accepted, then asks them to accept a value, which must be the reported one if there was any. Running it for every slot of a log, with a stable leader, gives Multi-Paxos. It is correct and efficient, but notoriously hard to turn into a complete system, which is why Raft was designed.
:::

:::

::: context multiraft Many small consensus groups
One Raft group can commit perhaps tens of thousands of commands a second, all through one leader. Databases such as CockroachDB and TiKV split their key space into ranges of about a hundred megabytes and run a separate Raft group for each range, with leaders spread over all the machines. Moving a range, or splitting it when it grows, is itself done through its group's log. Thousands of groups share each machine, so heartbeats are batched between machine pairs.
:::

::: context bft When nodes can lie
Byzantine fault-tolerant consensus tolerates servers that send wrong or contradictory messages, not just ones that stop. It needs 3f + 1 servers to tolerate f such faults, instead of 2f + 1, and more rounds of messages: every server must check what the others were told. PBFT, by Miguel Castro and Barbara Liskov in 1999, made it practical. Blockchains and some redundant flight computers use it, where a radiation upset or an attacker can make a unit produce wrong results.
:::
--- task
Write the two rules at the heart of Raft's log replication. A log is a list of entries `(term, command)`; Raft's index i is the list position i − 1, and index 0 means "before the first entry".

`append_entries(log, commit, prev_index, prev_term, entries, leader_commit)` is a follower handling an AppendEntries from the current leader (term checks are already done). `commit` is the follower's commit index. Return a tuple `(success, new_log, new_commit)`, without changing `log`:

- If `prev_index` is larger than the log's length, or `prev_index > 0` and the entry at index `prev_index` has a term other than `prev_term`, reject: return `False`, a copy of the log, and the unchanged commit index.
- Otherwise, go through `entries`; the k-th of them (from 0) belongs at index `prev_index + 1 + k`. If the log already has an entry there with the **same term**, keep it. At the first one with a **different term**, delete that entry and all after it, then append the remaining new entries. If the log is too short, append.
- If `leader_commit` is larger than `commit`, the new commit index is the smaller of `leader_commit` and `prev_index + len(entries)`; otherwise it is `commit`.

`leader_commit(log, match_index, current_term, commit)` is the leader deciding its commit index. `match_index` lists, for every server including the leader, the highest index known to be on it. Return the largest index N greater than `commit` such that a majority of the servers (more than half of `len(match_index)`) have `match_index` at least N, **and** the entry at index N has term `current_term`. If there is none, return `commit`.

For example, `append_entries([(1, "a"), (1, "b"), (2, "c")], 1, 2, 1, [(3, "x")], 3)` is `(True, [(1, "a"), (1, "b"), (3, "x")], 3)`, and `leader_commit([(1, "a"), (2, "b"), (4, "c")], [3, 2, 2, 1, 1], 4, 1)` is `1`: entry 2 is on a majority but from an old term, and entry 3 is on only one server.
--- starter
def append_entries(log, commit, prev_index, prev_term, entries, leader_commit):
    return True, log[:prev_index] + list(entries), leader_commit


def leader_commit(log, match_index, current_term, commit):
    return sorted(match_index)[len(match_index) // 2]
--- solution
def append_entries(log, commit, prev_index, prev_term, entries, leader_commit):
    if prev_index > len(log) or (prev_index > 0 and log[prev_index - 1][0] != prev_term):
        return False, list(log), commit
    new_log = list(log)
    for k, entry in enumerate(entries):
        position = prev_index + k
        if position < len(new_log):
            if new_log[position][0] == entry[0]:
                continue
            del new_log[position:]
        new_log.append(entry)
    if leader_commit > commit:
        commit = min(leader_commit, prev_index + len(entries))
    return True, new_log, commit


def leader_commit(log, match_index, current_term, commit):
    majority = len(match_index) // 2 + 1
    best = commit
    for n in range(commit + 1, len(log) + 1):
        on_majority = sum(1 for m in match_index if m >= n) >= majority
        if on_majority and log[n - 1][0] == current_term:
            best = n
    return best
--- hint
The consistency check comes first. Remember the shift: the entry at Raft index i is `log[i - 1]`, and the k-th new entry goes to list position `prev_index + k`.
--- hint
Copy the log, then walk the new entries. A matching term means "already have it": skip. A different term means a conflict: `del new_log[position:]` and append. Past the end, just append. Never delete when everything matches.
--- hint
For `leader_commit`, try every N from `commit + 1` up to the log's length, and keep the largest that is on a majority and has the current term. The starter's median trick finds the majority index, but ignores the term rule.
--- check case | append_entries: the example from the task

=> (True, [(1, 'a'), (1, 'b'), (3, 'x')], 3)

=> (True, [(1, 'a'), (1, 'b')], 1)
--- check case | prev_index past the end: reject

=> (False, [(1, 'a')], 0)

append_entries([(1, "a"), (2, "b")], 1, 2, 3, [(3, "c")], 2)
=> (False, [(1, 'a'), (2, 'b')], 1)
?? The follower's entry 2 has term 2, not 3, so the logs disagree there.
--- check case | A heartbeat: no entries, commit moves up to prev_index at most

=> (True, [(1, 'a'), (1, 'b'), (1, 'c')], 2)

=> 1

=> 4300

+++ question | What FLP rules out
--- ask
What does the FLP impossibility result say?
--- choice
Consensus is impossible whenever any process can crash.
--- choice correct
In an asynchronous system, no deterministic algorithm can guarantee that consensus terminates in every run if even one process may crash; algorithms can still be always safe and terminate when timing is good.
--- choice
Consensus needs at least 3f + 1 processes to tolerate f crashes.
--- choice
Consensus is impossible over a network that loses messages, however many retries are made.
--- why
FLP is about guarantees in the purely asynchronous model: an adversary that delays messages can keep the system undecided forever. Raft and Paxos never violate agreement, and they decide once the network is timely for long enough. 3f + 1 is the bound for Byzantine faults, and the lossy-link result is the two generals.

+++ question | Why random timeouts
--- ask
Why does Raft pick each server's election timeout at random, for example between 150 and 300 ms?
--- choice
To spread the load of heartbeats evenly.
--- choice
Because a leader must be chosen at random for fairness.
--- choice correct
So that one follower usually times out clearly before the others and collects a majority of votes before anyone else becomes a candidate, making split votes rare.
--- choice
To make the algorithm correct: with equal timeouts two leaders could be elected in one term.
--- why
Safety never depends on the timeouts: each server votes once per term, so two leaders in one term are impossible. Randomness is for liveness: if all followers timed out together, they would all vote for themselves and split the vote again and again.

+++ question | The old entry on a majority
--- ask
A Raft leader in term 4 sees that an entry from term 2, at index 7, is now stored on a majority of servers. Why must it not mark index 7 committed yet?
--- choice
Because entries can only be committed by the leader that created them.
--- choice correct
Because a server whose log ends in an entry from term 3 could still win an election and overwrite index 7; only once an entry from term 4 is on a majority is that impossible.
--- choice
Because commit indexes must increase by one at a time.
--- choice
Because the followers have not applied the entry yet.
--- why
Voters compare last-entry terms. A majority that holds only the term-2 entry would still vote for a candidate whose last entry is from term 3, and that candidate's log wins. A term-4 entry on a majority blocks such candidates, and it commits everything before it by Log Matching.

+++ practice | Granting a vote
--- task
Write `handle_vote(state, req)`, a Raft server handling a RequestVote. `state` is a dictionary with keys `"term"`, `"voted_for"` (a server name, or `None`) and `"log"` (the list of the terms of its entries, oldest first). `req` has keys `"term"`, `"candidate"`, `"last_index"` and `"last_term"` (the index and term of the candidate's last entry, 0 and 0 for an empty log).

Return a tuple `(granted, new_state)`, without changing `state`:

1. If `req["term"]` is smaller than the server's term, deny.
2. If it is larger, the server first adopts it and forgets its vote (`voted_for` becomes `None`).
3. Then grant the vote if the server has not voted in this term, or already voted for this same candidate, **and** the candidate's log is at least as up-to-date: its last term is larger than the server's last term (0 for an empty log), or the last terms are equal and its last index is at least the server's log length. Granting records the candidate in `voted_for`.

For example, `handle_vote({"term": 2, "voted_for": None, "log": [1, 2]}, {"term": 3, "candidate": "s4", "last_index": 2, "last_term": 2})` is `(True, {"term": 3, "voted_for": "s4", "log": [1, 2]})`.
--- starter
def handle_vote(state, req):
    s = dict(state)
    if req["term"] >= s["term"]:
        s["term"] = req["term"]
        s["voted_for"] = req["candidate"]
        return True, s
    return False, s
--- solution
def handle_vote(state, req):
    s = dict(state)
    if req["term"] < s["term"]:
        return False, s
    if req["term"] > s["term"]:
        s["term"] = req["term"]
        s["voted_for"] = None
    my_last_term = s["log"][-1] if s["log"] else 0
    up_to_date = req["last_term"] > my_last_term or (req["last_term"] == my_last_term and req["last_index"] >= len(s["log"]))
    if s["voted_for"] in (None, req["candidate"]) and up_to_date:
        s["voted_for"] = req["candidate"]
        return True, s
    return False, s
--- hint
Copy the state with `dict(state)` and work on the copy. Handle the smaller term first, then adopt a larger one.
--- hint
"Up-to-date" compares last terms first; only when they are equal does the length decide. A longer log with an older last term loses.
--- check case | The example from the task
handle_vote({"term": 2, "voted_for": None, "log": [1, 2]}, {"term": 3, "candidate": "s4", "last_index": 2, "last_term": 2})
=> (True, {'term': 3, 'voted_for': 's4', 'log': [1, 2]})
--- check case | An old term is denied, and nothing changes
handle_vote({"term": 5, "voted_for": None, "log": []}, {"term": 4, "candidate": "s2", "last_index": 9, "last_term": 4})
=> (False, {'term': 5, 'voted_for': None, 'log': []})
--- check case | Already voted for someone else in this term
handle_vote({"term": 3, "voted_for": "s1", "log": [1]}, {"term": 3, "candidate": "s2", "last_index": 1, "last_term": 1})
=> (False, {'term': 3, 'voted_for': 's1', 'log': [1]})
--- check case | Asking again for the same candidate is granted
handle_vote({"term": 3, "voted_for": "s2", "log": [1]}, {"term": 3, "candidate": "s2", "last_index": 1, "last_term": 1})
=> (True, {'term': 3, 'voted_for': 's2', 'log': [1]})
--- check case | A longer log with an older last term is not up-to-date
handle_vote({"term": 4, "voted_for": None, "log": [1, 3]}, {"term": 5, "candidate": "s3", "last_index": 6, "last_term": 2})
=> (False, {'term': 5, 'voted_for': None, 'log': [1, 3]})
?? Compare the last terms first: 2 is older than 3, so the length does not matter. The server still adopts term 5.
--- check case | Same last term, shorter log: denied
handle_vote({"term": 2, "voted_for": None, "log": [1, 2, 2]}, {"term": 2, "candidate": "s5", "last_index": 2, "last_term": 2})
=> (False, {'term': 2, 'voted_for': None, 'log': [1, 2, 2]})
--- check case | Empty logs are equally up-to-date
handle_vote({"term": 0, "voted_for": None, "log": []}, {"term": 1, "candidate": "s1", "last_index": 0, "last_term": 0})
=> (True, {'term': 1, 'voted_for': 's1', 'log': []})
--- check test | Does not change the state it was given
(lambda s: (handle_vote(s, {"term": 9, "candidate": "x", "last_index": 0, "last_term": 0}), s == {"term": 1, "voted_for": None, "log": []})[1])({"term": 1, "voted_for": None, "log": []})

+++ practice | Checking the Log Matching Property
--- task
The Log Matching Property says: if two logs have an entry with the same index and term, then the logs are identical in all entries before that index. A bug in a replication layer can break it, and a test harness should notice.

Each log is a list of the terms of its entries (index i is position i − 1). Write `matching_violations(logs)` that returns every triple `(a, b, i)` with a < b and i a Raft index (from 1), such that logs a and b both have an entry at index i, with the same term, but differ somewhere at an index below i. Sort the triples by a, then b, then i.

For example, `matching_violations([[1, 1, 2], [1, 3, 2]])` is `[(0, 1, 3)]`: both logs have term 2 at index 3, but they differ at index 2.
--- starter
def matching_violations(logs):
    out = []
    for a in range(len(logs)):
        for b in range(a + 1, len(logs)):
            if logs[a] != logs[b]:
                out.append((a, b, 1))
    return out
--- solution
def matching_violations(logs):
    out = []
    for a in range(len(logs)):
        for b in range(a + 1, len(logs)):
            differ_before = False
            for k in range(min(len(logs[a]), len(logs[b]))):
                if logs[a][k] == logs[b][k] and differ_before:
                    out.append((a, b, k + 1))
                if logs[a][k] != logs[b][k]:
                    differ_before = True
    return out
--- hint
Compare every pair of logs, position by position, up to the shorter length. Logs of different lengths are fine: only indexes both logs have count.
--- hint
Walk forward keeping a flag "they have already differed". An index where the terms are equal while the flag is set is a violation.
--- check case | The example from the task
matching_violations([[1, 1, 2], [1, 3, 2]])
=> [(0, 1, 3)]
--- check case | A healthy cluster: a prefix and a divergent tail are both fine
matching_violations([[1, 1, 2, 3], [1, 1, 2], [1, 1, 4, 4, 4]])
=> []
--- check case | Several violations and several pairs
matching_violations([[1, 2, 2, 2], [1, 3, 2, 2], [1, 2, 2, 2]])
=> [(0, 1, 3), (0, 1, 4), (1, 2, 3), (1, 2, 4)]
--- check case | No logs, one log, and empty logs
(matching_violations([]), matching_violations([[1, 2]]), matching_violations([[], [1]]))
=> ([], [], [])
--- check case | Differing at index 1 taints everything after it
matching_violations([[1, 5, 5], [2, 5, 5]])
=> [(0, 1, 2), (0, 1, 3)]

+++ practice | Repairing a follower's log
--- task
When a follower's log disagrees with the leader's, the leader repairs it by backing up. Logs here are lists of terms (index i is position i − 1). The leader starts with `next_index = len(leader_log) + 1` and repeats:

- send an AppendEntries with `prev_index = next_index - 1`, `prev_term` the leader's term at that index (0 when `prev_index` is 0), and as entries every leader entry from index `next_index` on;
- the follower applies Raft's rules: reject if it has no entry at `prev_index` with term `prev_term` (index 0 always matches); otherwise keep entries whose term matches, and at the first conflict delete the rest of its log and append the remaining new entries;
- if it rejected, `next_index` goes down by 1 and the leader tries again; if it accepted, the repair is done.

Write `repair(leader_log, follower_log)` that returns a tuple `(messages, new_follower_log)`: the number of AppendEntries sent and the follower's log afterwards. Do not change the arguments.

For example, `repair([1, 1, 2, 3, 3], [1, 1, 1, 1, 1, 1])` is `(4, [1, 1, 2, 3, 3])`: prev indexes 5, 4 and 3 are rejected, and prev index 2 (term 1) matches.

After the repair the follower's log starts with the whole leader log, but it may be longer: when every entry sent matches, nothing is deleted, as in the lesson.
--- starter
def repair(leader_log, follower_log):
    return 1, list(leader_log)
--- solution
def follower_append(log, prev_index, prev_term, entries):
    if prev_index > len(log) or (prev_index > 0 and log[prev_index - 1] != prev_term):
        return False, log
    new_log = list(log)
    for k, term in enumerate(entries):
        position = prev_index + k
        if position < len(new_log):
            if new_log[position] == term:
                continue
            del new_log[position:]
        new_log.append(term)
    return True, new_log


def repair(leader_log, follower_log):
    next_index = len(leader_log) + 1
    log = list(follower_log)
    messages = 0
    while True:
        prev_index = next_index - 1
        prev_term = leader_log[prev_index - 1] if prev_index > 0 else 0
        messages += 1
        ok, log = follower_append(log, prev_index, prev_term, leader_log[prev_index:])
        if ok:
            return messages, log
        next_index -= 1
--- hint
Write the follower's side as its own function, the same rules as in the lesson's task but on lists of terms.
--- hint
The loop always ends: at `prev_index` 0 the check cannot fail. Count a message for every attempt, including the one that succeeds.
--- check case | The example from the task
repair([1, 1, 2, 3, 3], [1, 1, 1, 1, 1, 1])
=> (4, [1, 1, 2, 3, 3])
--- check case | Identical logs: one message
repair([1, 2, 2], [1, 2, 2])
=> (1, [1, 2, 2])
--- check case | An empty follower
repair([1, 1, 3], [])
=> (4, [1, 1, 3])
--- check case | A follower that is only behind
repair([1, 1, 2, 2], [1, 1])
=> (3, [1, 1, 2, 2])
--- check case | Nothing in common: back to the start
repair([2, 2], [1, 1, 1])
=> (3, [2, 2])
--- check case | A follower with extra matching entries keeps them
repair([4, 4], [4, 4, 4])
=> (1, [4, 4, 4])
--- check case | A long conflicting tail: one message per step back
(lambda res: (res[0], res[1] == [1] * 100 + [3] * 50))(repair([1] * 100 + [3] * 50, [1] * 100 + [2] * 200))
=> (51, True)

+++ practice | Applying committed entries
--- task
A Raft server applies committed entries to its state machine, in index order, each exactly once. The state machine is a key-value store: a command is `("set", key, value)` or `("del", key)` (deleting a missing key does nothing). `log` is the server's list of entries `(term, command)`, and `commits` is the sequence of commit indexes the server learned, in order.

Messages can be delayed, so a commit index may be **lower** than one learned before; it must not undo anything. A commit index can also be **higher** than the log's length (the leader knows about entries this server has not received); then the server applies only what it has.

Write `apply_committed(log, commits)` that starts from an empty store, handles each commit index in turn, applying every entry after the last applied one up to it, and returns a tuple `(store, applied)`: the final dictionary and the number of entries applied.

For example, `apply_committed([(1, ("set", "a", 1)), (1, ("set", "b", 2)), (2, ("del", "a"))], [2, 1, 5])` is `({"b": 2}, 3)`.
--- starter
def apply_committed(log, commits):
    store = {}
    for term, command in log[:commits[-1]]:
        if command[0] == "set":
            store[command[1]] = command[2]
    return store, commits[-1]
--- solution
def apply_committed(log, commits):
    store = {}
    applied = 0
    for commit in commits:
        target = min(commit, len(log))
        while applied < target:
            command = log[applied][1]
            if command[0] == "set":
                store[command[1]] = command[2]
            else:
                store.pop(command[1], None)
            applied += 1
    return store, applied
--- hint
Keep `applied`, the number of entries already applied, and never move it backwards.
--- hint
For each commit index, the target is the smaller of it and the log's length; apply entries while `applied` is below the target. `store.pop(key, None)` deletes without failing on a missing key.
--- check case | The example from the task
apply_committed([(1, ("set", "a", 1)), (1, ("set", "b", 2)), (2, ("del", "a"))], [2, 1, 5])
=> ({'b': 2}, 3)
--- check case | No commits, and an empty log
(apply_committed([(1, ("set", "a", 1))], []), apply_committed([], [3]))
=> (({}, 0), ({}, 0))
--- check case | A lower commit index undoes nothing
apply_committed([(1, ("set", "x", 1)), (1, ("set", "x", 2))], [2, 0, 1])
=> ({'x': 2}, 2)
--- check case | Deleting a key that is not there
apply_committed([(1, ("del", "ghost")), (1, ("set", "k", "v"))], [2])
=> ({'k': 'v'}, 2)
--- check case | The same commit index twice applies once
apply_committed([(1, ("set", "n", 1)), (2, ("set", "n", 2)), (2, ("set", "m", 3))], [1, 1, 3, 3])
=> ({'n': 2, 'm': 3}, 3)
--- check case | Commit index 0
apply_committed([(1, ("set", "a", 1))], [0])
=> ({}, 0)

+++ practice | Debug: the commit rule from Figure 8
--- task
The starter's `commit_index(log, match_index, current_term, commit)` decides a Raft leader's commit index. `log` is a list of `(term, command)` entries, and `match_index` lists, for every server including the leader, the highest index known to be on it. It should return the largest index N above `commit` that is stored on a majority **and** holds an entry of the current term, or `commit` if there is none.

It commits the entry of Figure 8 that a later leader can still overwrite. Find the bug and fix it.
--- starter
def commit_index(log, match_index, current_term, commit):
    majority = len(match_index) // 2 + 1
    best = commit
    for n in range(commit + 1, len(log) + 1):
        if sum(1 for m in match_index if m >= n) >= majority:
            best = n
    return best
--- solution
def commit_index(log, match_index, current_term, commit):
    majority = len(match_index) // 2 + 1
    best = commit
    for n in range(commit + 1, len(log) + 1):
        if sum(1 for m in match_index if m >= n) >= majority and log[n - 1][0] == current_term:
            best = n
    return best
--- hint
Which of the two conditions in the task does the loop check?
--- hint
An entry on a majority may be committed by counting only if its term is the leader's current term.
--- check case | Figure 8: the old-term entry on a majority is not committed
commit_index([(1, "a"), (2, "b")], [2, 2, 2, 1, 1], 4, 1)
=> 1
--- check case | After a term-4 entry reaches the majority, both commit
commit_index([(1, "a"), (2, "b"), (4, "c")], [3, 3, 3, 1, 2], 4, 1)
=> 3
--- check case | Current-term entries commit as soon as a majority has them
commit_index([(3, "a"), (3, "b"), (3, "c")], [3, 2, 1], 3, 0)
=> 2
--- check case | Nothing on a majority
commit_index([(5, "a")], [1, 0, 0], 5, 0)
=> 0

+++ practice | Electing a leader
--- task
Simulate Raft's leader election in discrete time steps, with no logs (every candidate is up-to-date). There are `n` servers, 0 to n − 1, all followers in term 0 with no vote. Server i's election timeouts are the list `timeouts[i]`; it uses them in order, starting again from the first when the list runs out. Its first deadline is `timeouts[i][0]`. Every message takes `d` steps. Servers in the set `down` never do anything.

At each step t = 1, 2, …, `limit`:

1. **Deliver** the messages arriving at t, sorted by (sender, receiver, kind, term). A down receiver ignores them.
   - A **vote request** of term T from candidate c at server j: if T is larger than j's term, j adopts T, forgets its vote and becomes a follower. Then, if T equals j's term and j has not voted or voted for c, j votes for c, sets its next deadline to t plus its next timeout, and sends a **yes** reply of term T to c.
   - A **yes** of term T from j at server c: if c is a candidate in term T, it counts j's vote. With votes from a majority (more than n / 2, counting itself), c becomes leader: return `(t, c, T)`.
2. **Time out**: every server that is up, and whose deadline is t, in increasing order, becomes a candidate: its term goes up by 1, it votes for itself, sets its next deadline to t plus its next timeout, and sends a vote request of its new term to every other server. (If its own vote is already a majority, it becomes leader at once.)

Write `election(n, timeouts, d, down, limit)` that returns `(t, leader, term)` for the first leader, or `None` if there is none by step `limit`.

For example, `election(3, [[5], [8], [9]], 1, set(), 50)` is `(7, 0, 1)`: server 0 times out at 5, its requests arrive at 6, and the yes votes arrive at 7.
--- starter
def election(n, timeouts, d, down, limit):
    first = min(i for i in range(n) if i not in down)
    return timeouts[first][0] + 2 * d, first, 1
--- solution
def election(n, timeouts, d, down, limit):
    term = [0] * n
    voted = [None] * n
    role = ["follower"] * n
    votes = [set() for _ in range(n)]
    used = [1] * n
    deadline = [timeouts[i][0] for i in range(n)]
    inbox = {}
    majority = n // 2 + 1

    def reset(i, t):
        deadline[i] = t + timeouts[i][used[i] % len(timeouts[i])]
        used[i] += 1

    for t in range(1, limit + 1):
        for sender, receiver, kind, T in sorted(inbox.pop(t, [])):
            if receiver in down:
                continue
            if kind == "request":
                if T > term[receiver]:
                    term[receiver] = T
                    voted[receiver] = None
                    role[receiver] = "follower"
                if T == term[receiver] and voted[receiver] in (None, sender):
                    voted[receiver] = sender
                    reset(receiver, t)
                    inbox.setdefault(t + d, []).append((receiver, sender, "yes", T))
            elif role[receiver] == "candidate" and term[receiver] == T:
                votes[receiver].add(sender)
                if len(votes[receiver]) >= majority:
                    return t, receiver, T
        for i in range(n):
            if i in down or deadline[i] != t:
                continue
            term[i] += 1
            role[i] = "candidate"
            voted[i] = i
            votes[i] = {i}
            if len(votes[i]) >= majority:
                return t, i, term[i]
            reset(i, t)
            for j in range(n):
                if j != i:
                    inbox.setdefault(t + d, []).append((i, j, "request", term[i]))
    return None
--- hint
Keep per-server lists: term, vote, role, the set of votes received, the next deadline and how many timeouts it has used. Keep messages in a dictionary from arrival step to a list of tuples `(sender, receiver, kind, term)`, so sorting the list gives the delivery order.
--- hint
Each step, deliver first, then handle timeouts. The next timeout of server i is `timeouts[i][used % len(timeouts[i])]`, with `used` counting the timeouts taken so far (1 after the first deadline).
--- hint
A candidate that sees a larger term becomes a follower, so its old votes stop counting: check both "is a candidate" and "the reply's term is my term" before counting a yes.
--- check case | The example from the task
election(3, [[5], [8], [9]], 1, set(), 50)
=> (7, 0, 1)
--- check case | A single server elects itself at its first timeout
election(1, [[4]], 1, set(), 10)
=> (4, 0, 1)
--- check case | Two candidates at once: the other voters see the lower id's request first
election(3, [[5], [5], [9]], 1, set(), 50)
=> (7, 0, 1)
--- check case | A split vote with one server down, settled in term 2
election(4, [[5, 20], [5, 3], [9, 9], [9]], 1, {3}, 60)
=> (10, 1, 2)
?? At step 5 servers 0 and 1 both stand for term 1. Server 2 votes for 0, which then has 2 votes of the 3 it needs. Server 1 times out again at step 8 and wins term 2.
--- check case | Without a majority up, nobody wins
election(3, [[2], [3], [4]], 1, {1, 2}, 40)
=> None
--- check case | A slow network, with timeouts longer than a round trip
election(3, [[10], [20], [30]], 3, set(), 60)
=> (16, 0, 1)
--- check case | Timeouts no longer than a round trip: no election ever finishes
election(3, [[4], [6], [30]], 3, set(), 60)
=> None
?? Server 0's timeout (4) is shorter than the round trip (6): it starts a new term before its votes come back, and its old votes no longer count.
--- check case | Five servers with jittered timeouts
election(5, [[150 + (37 * i * k) % 150 for k in range(1, 6)] for i in range(5)], 10, set(), 2000)
=> (170, 0, 1)

=== par-12 | MapReduce and dataflow: parallel data processing on a cluster that fails
--- teach
Lesson 3 met map and reduce as patterns on one machine, and lessons 8 to 11 showed what happens when machines fail and messages go astray. This lesson joins the two. **MapReduce**, published by Jeffrey Dean and Sanjay Ghemawat at Google in 2004, lets a programmer who writes two small functions process terabytes on thousands of unreliable machines, without writing a single line about threads, messages or failures. You will build its engine over a simulated cluster, crashes included, and then see how **dataflow** systems such as Spark generalise it.

### The programming model

The programmer writes two functions:

- **map** takes one input record and **emits** any number of intermediate pairs (key, value);
- **reduce** takes one key and the list of **all** values emitted for that key, anywhere, and produces the result for that key.

The system does everything in between: it runs map on every record, groups the intermediate pairs by key (the **shuffle**), and runs reduce once per key. For example, to find how many telemetry frames each spacecraft sent per day, from log lines such as `"2026-03-01 SAT-7 frame 4411"`:

```python
def frames_map(line):
    day, craft, _, _ = line.split()
    yield (craft, day), 1

def frames_reduce(key, counts):
    return sum(counts)

print(list(frames_map("2026-03-01 SAT-7 frame 4411")))    # [(('SAT-7', '2026-03-01'), 1)]
print(frames_reduce(("SAT-7", "2026-03-01"), [1, 1, 1]))   # 3
```

The model's strength is what it leaves out. Map calls share nothing, so they can run anywhere, in any order, as often as needed. Reduce calls for different keys share nothing either. That independence is what makes both parallelism and fault tolerance easy.

### How a job runs

A **master** (coordinator) node splits the input into M pieces, typically 64 MB each, and creates **M map tasks** and **R reduce tasks**, which it hands out to worker nodes as they become free:

1. **Map phase.** A map task reads its split, calls map on every record, and divides the emitted pairs into R **partitions** by the key: partition = hash(key) mod R. It writes each partition to its local disk, sorted by key.
2. **Shuffle.** Reduce task r fetches partition r from **every** map task's output: M × R transfers in all, across the network.
3. **Reduce phase.** The reduce task merges what it fetched, sorts it by key so that equal keys are next to each other, calls reduce once per key with that key's values, and writes its output file.

Every pair with a given key goes to the same partition, so exactly one reduce call sees all of that key's values. That is why the hash must give the same answer on every machine. Python's built-in `hash` of a string does not: it is [[randomised per process|hash-seed]]. So partitioners use a fixed hash function such as CRC-32:

```python
import zlib

for word in ["orbit", "apogee"]:
    print(word, zlib.crc32(word.encode()) % 4)   # the same on every machine, every run
```

### Combiners: reducing before the shuffle

In a word count, a map task over a big document emits ("the", 1) thousands of times, and every one of those pairs crosses the network. A **combiner** is a reduce run on each map task's own output, before the shuffle: the map task sends ("the", 2734) once instead.

A combiner is only correct when running reduce on parts, then on the results, gives the same answer as running it once on everything. That holds when the operation is **associative and commutative** (lesson 3), and when the combiner's output has the same form as the mapper's values, because the reducer will receive a mixture of both. Sum, count, maximum and minimum qualify. An **average** does not: the average of averages is wrong when the parts have different sizes. The fix is to carry the pair (sum, count), which does combine, and divide only in the reducer.

### Failures: re-execution

On a thousand machines, some fail during every long job. MapReduce handles it without any consensus protocol, using one idea: **tasks are deterministic, so a failed task can simply run again.**

- The master pings workers. If a worker stops answering (lesson 8's failure detector), its **in-progress** tasks are given to others.
- Its **completed map tasks** are run again too, because their output sat on the dead machine's local disk. Completed reduce tasks are not: their output is already in the replicated file system.
- A task writes to a temporary file and **renames** it into place when it finishes. A rename is atomic, so readers see a whole output or none, and if two copies of a task finish, one rename simply replaces the other with identical data. The master ignores a second "done" message for a task it already counts as done.

This is lesson 8's deduplication in another form: the effect of each task happens exactly once, because running it twice yields the same thing, and only one result is kept. It requires map and reduce to be **deterministic** and free of side effects, such as sending emails or incrementing an external counter.

**Correctness.** With deterministic functions, the output equals that of some serial execution, whatever crashed: each successful task attempt produces exactly what any other attempt would have, the master uses exactly one successful attempt per task, and the reduce side sees each map task's output once.

### Stragglers and backup tasks

A job finishes when its **last** task does. One slow machine, with a failing disk or too many other jobs, holds up everything: lesson 1's serial part in disguise. Near the end of a phase, the master starts **backup copies** of the tasks still running, and takes whichever copy finishes first. Dean and Ghemawat reported that a large sort took 44% longer with backup tasks switched off. Again, this is only safe because tasks are deterministic.

### What it costs

- **Work** is the sum of all map calls and reduce calls, plus the shuffle, plus sorting: about O(n log n) for n intermediate pairs, spread over the workers.
- **The shuffle is usually the bottleneck:** every intermediate pair may cross the network, and the M × R fetches all happen at once. Combiners exist to shrink it.
- **Load balance.** M and R are set much larger than the number of workers, often M in the hundreds of thousands, so that fast workers take more tasks and a failure costs only a little work: lesson 2's parallel slackness.
- **Skew.** All values of one key go to one reduce call. If one key is hugely common, its reducer runs far longer than the rest, and by Amdahl's law the job waits for it. The cure is to split hot keys, for example by adding a random suffix and combining the pieces in a second job.

### Dataflow: beyond two phases

Many real computations are chains of MapReduce jobs, and each job writes its whole output to disk before the next one reads it. Iterative algorithms such as PageRank or machine learning repeat the same step dozens of times, and pay that cost each time. **Dataflow** systems describe the whole computation as a graph of operators instead: map, filter, join, group, aggregate.

**Apache Spark** is the best known. A dataset is split into partitions, and every transformation records how it was computed from its parents: its **lineage**. Spark distinguishes two kinds of dependencies:

- **narrow:** each output partition needs only one input partition (map, filter), so the steps can be chained on one machine without moving data;
- **wide:** an output partition needs data from many input partitions (group by key, join), which requires a shuffle.

The graph is cut into [[stages|lineage-picture]] at the wide dependencies, and each stage runs as a set of parallel tasks. Data between steps can stay in memory, which makes iterative jobs many times faster than chains of MapReduce jobs. Fault tolerance keeps the MapReduce idea: a lost partition is **recomputed** from its lineage rather than restored from a copy. **Streaming** systems such as Apache Flink and Google's Dataflow run the same kind of graph continuously over data that never ends, grouping it by time into [[windows|windows]].

### Where it is used

- **Google** built its web index with MapReduce for years; **Hadoop**, the open-source version, ran the data warehouses of Yahoo and Facebook.
- **Spark** runs batch analytics, SQL and machine learning in most large companies today; SQL engines such as BigQuery and Presto compile queries into the same kind of stage graphs.
- **Earth observation:** processing years of satellite images into maps, as Google Earth Engine does, is map-heavy work over tiles with a reduce per region.
- **Mission telemetry:** a constellation's daily housekeeping data, grouped by spacecraft and subsystem, is a MapReduce job in shape, and streaming dataflows watch it live for anomalies.

**Watch out:**

- **Non-deterministic or side-effecting map and reduce functions** break re-execution: a retried task can produce different output, or send the email twice.
- **A combiner must not change the meaning.** The average of averages is not the average. Carry (sum, count).
- **The partitioner must be the same everywhere.** Never partition with Python's `hash` of strings across processes.
- **Grouping needs sorting first.** `itertools.groupby` groups only neighbouring equal keys; unsorted pairs give several groups for one key.
- **Watch for skew.** One hot key makes one reducer the whole job's critical path.

::: context hash-seed Why Python's hash changes between runs
Since Python 3.3, the hash of a `str` or `bytes` is salted with a random value chosen when the interpreter starts, unless the environment variable PYTHONHASHSEED fixes it. The salt defends web servers against an attack in which a client sends many keys chosen to collide in a dict, making every lookup slow. The price is that two Python processes compute different hashes for the same string, so a partitioner that uses `hash` would send the same key to different reducers from different mappers. Integers are not salted: `hash(7)` is always 7.
:::

::: context lineage-picture Stages cut at shuffles
A job that reads telemetry, filters bad frames, counts per spacecraft and joins with a table of owners. The filter is narrow and stays with the read, in stage 1. Counting per key needs a shuffle, which starts stage 2; the join needs another, which starts stage 3.

